## REVIEW

# O'Connor movement review

Read the complete supplied movement in chapter order before judging it. State
whether this is self-review, a fresh-context simulated cold read, or a real human
read. Do not claim independent testing when you have the drafting history.

First give an unscored reader response: where interest caught, where it slipped,
which person matters, which event you expect next, and whether you would continue.
Then use two perspectives: a fluent thirteen-year-old reader and an adult genre
reader. These are lenses, not demographic research. Do not infer a sales forecast.

For each applicable dimension give a 1–10 score, an exact passage location, a reason
and confidence. Mark an absent feature N/A instead of giving invented evidence:

| Dimension | What to judge |
|---|---|
| Opening pull | A concrete reason to care before explanation takes over |
| Keep reading | Unresolved pressure and satisfying local payoff |
| Interest / freshness | Specific people, choices and problems |
| Clarity / flow | Meaning on one pass and clean transitions |
| Character attachment | Agency, vulnerability, recognizable differences |
| Humor / warmth | Character-grounded pleasure, timing, emotional honesty |
| Action / suspense | Trackable contest, adaptation, cost, earned scene length |
| Progression payoff | Effort visibly changes capability and consequence |
| Connection | State, plants and relationships carried across chapters |
| Read-aloud quality | Punctuation, referents, dialogue attribution and cadence |

Anchor interpretation: 5 = understandable but inconsistent; 7 = engaging with
located weaknesses; 9 = compelling with few substantial distractions. These are
editorial judgments, not calibrated measurements. Do not average the two readers
into a release gate. Do not award points for matching another book's word counts.

Separately check canon, physical state, power limits, knowledge boundaries and
reserved disclosures. Cite the project evidence for a contradiction. If only the
plan supports a claim, identify it as plan adherence rather than a canon error.

Return a concise repair brief with at most three priorities. Each needs location,
observed issue, effect on the reader, proposed scope and a strength to preserve.
Retain the developed action the owner requested. Punctuation can repair an unclear
sentence; do not shorten a successful fight merely to reduce its word count.

If comparing runs, keep chapter coverage and scoring rubric equal. Hide model and
seat labels from a fresh reader where feasible. Report tradeoffs and uncertainty;
one movement does not prove which seat is better across a series.

---

## REVIEW COVERAGE

8 files supplied in declared order. Only claim this coverage. If the movement is incomplete, say so. Editorial canon evidence is present only when supplied below. Without it, report apparent inconsistency, not verified canon violations.

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-23.md

# Chapter 23 — Round by the Ferry

The mule went over the top of the brown hill, and after that there was nothing on the north road for him to watch.

Cael stayed where he was, on the gravel at the water's edge, with the book shut on his knee and his left arm held in against five bands of linen. It was the start of something, and so he took the inventory, as he took it at the start of anything. Three ribs were broken on the left side, low; they had been taped on the eighty-first day, and the tape was to come off on the ninety-ninth and not before. The calf was bound in a healer's cloth and was closing by itself, as his hurts did. Lira had a shin that was four days old, and an ankle that no healer on this road was going to mend, and one boot. Brom's left wrist wore a linen cuff, and he was to hang nothing from it until the middle of the eighty-seventh day. Karis had been told to ask nothing of herself for three days more, and she was asleep against a box.

There were three boxes, and they stood on the bare ground by a gate-house wall. That was the item he liked least. A hundred and sixteen marks lay in Karis's purse, and six of them were a smith's. The plank where those six would be paid was six days down a road, and each box weighed as much as a grown sheep. He had one man who was allowed to carry, and four who were not fit to.

He was still turning it over when a stick came down the bank behind him, tapping.

It was the keeper. He let himself down onto a flat stone by stages, with the notched hazel across his knees, and he looked at the river for some time before he spoke, like a man who has all his life counted what crossed it.

"I've a cart goes out at first light," he said. "For grain."

"To Thornwater?"

"Where else is there grain? I'd meant him to go over the top, when the dry spell came, and be home in six days." The brown eyes came round. "You've told me what's over the top. So he goes round by the one-armed man's ferry, the same as his father went, and that's eight days out and eight home, and the frost won't wait on me while I think about it."

Cael said nothing. He had learned on a hill to let a carter come to the end of his own load.

"He's a lad and two mules. It's sixteen days of road, and I'd as soon he had somebody walking in front of him for the first six." The keeper tipped his stick at the gate-house wall. "Your boxes'd ride. There's room on the bed going out; it's coming home that she's full."

"What's the hire?"

"There's none. I've no paper for it and I'm not sending to a plank for some." He scratched his jaw with the stick's end. "You walked my hill for seventy and brought me a no. I'd have paid the seventy three times over in carts, by next thaw, if you'd brought me a yes to please me. I can find a bed for three boxes."

So there it was. Cael went through it once, from habit, for the place where it cost something, and he could not find one. A column on a pasted leaf had ended in the wrong word, and the five of them had written it anyway, and for that a man was offering him wheels for six days. He had been paid for a great many things in his life, and most of them had been somebody's convenience. He did not think that he had ever before been done a kindness on the strength of a no.

"I'll ask them," he said.

"Ask them. He goes at first light whether or not."

He asked them in the yard, by the well, with the light gone grey. Brom looked at three boxes and then at his own left wrist, and Cael watched him weigh a road of six days against a rule of three, and put his hand up. Lira's was up already. Seln lifted two fingers from his folded arms. They had to wake Karis for hers, and she raised it before her eyes were properly open and asked afterward what she had voted for.

"Wheels," said Lira.

"Whose?"

"His. They go round."

"Then I am in favour of it," said Karis, and went back to sleep.

They lay that night in the keeper's cart-shed, under a heap of empty grain sacks that smelled of last year's barley, since their own blankets were at the bottom of a gorge. It was warmer than a bay of shale had been. He lay on his right side, where the ribs hung, and listened to four people breathing, and counted nine days to a fence in a valley where a healer meant to put her hands on him again.

---

They went through the ford at first light on the eighty-fifth day, and nobody spoke until the far bank.

The carter was a long young man with a narrow sleepy face and the largest feet that Cael had ever seen on anybody. He was the keeper's sister's son. He had two grey mules, and a cart a foot wider in the bed than the one that lay in the gorge. In the six days that followed he said perhaps forty words, and most of them were to the mules. The red oxen drew his wheels over for nothing, since the wheels were the hold's own. He walked through behind them with his breeches rolled to the thigh and his boots hung round his neck by their laces.

The five waded after. It was a hundred yards of gravel under a foot of quick water, cold enough to make the bones of his feet ache. The tape told him where the edge was whenever a stone turned under him. He kept his breath shallow and his eyes on the cart's tail. Three boxes sat on it under a sheet of patched canvas, left and middle and right, with the corner that was worn white turned outward. On top of them, roped down by her own instructions, sat Karis, who had been lifted up there like a sack and had made no objection.

On the south bank he stopped to put his boots on, and saw the prints.

They were a day old. The dew had softened their edges, but the gravel gave way there to a strip of grey silt, and the silt had kept everything. Four people had come up out of the river in single file, each a crew's length behind the one in front, and two mules after them on one rein. He crouched, as far as five bands of linen would let him, and read it.

The first prints were a man's, of the middle size, set down evenly. Beside the right foot, here and there, was a small round dent, where the haft of a hand-axe had swung at his thigh and touched ground on the slope. The second walker had grounded a spear at every step. The third set of marks was shorter on the left side than on the right by the width of two fingers, every stride, and no deeper for it; whoever made them had been favouring that hip for so long that the body no longer argued. The fourth were the smallest feet of the four and went in the deepest.

"That's the boy," said Lira, over his shoulder. She was standing on one boot and one bare foot, with her arms wrapped round herself. "He's carrying more than the others. Look how his heels go in."

"More than his share."

"Somebody ought to stop him."

"I should think somebody does, about once a day." He stood up carefully. "They went up this bank at dusk, with the light going and a river to get over, and there isn't a scuffed print among the four of them. Nobody turned to look back. Nobody stepped out of the line."

He had not expected to enjoy it. But there was a pleasure in reading a thing well made, and he had it now, as he had had it from the east tier of a yard, watching a good pair work. The difference was that he did not want to beat this one. He had no notion what he wanted from it; the man had told him to be good enough to be glad of, and Cael was reading the silt to learn what *good enough* walked like.

The carter had halted his mules on the bank and was waiting, with his sleepy face turned toward them. He had seen Lira's foot. He looked at it for some time, and then he went round to the cart's tail and took down a pair of boots.

They were his spare ones. Each was as long as Cael's forearm.

"Two marks," said the carter.

It was the first thing that anybody had heard him say.

"For the one?" said Lira. "I've only the one foot wants it."

He considered that, and shook his head slowly. "They're a pair."

"I've got a left. Look at it; it's a perfectly good left. What am I to do with yours?"

"Keep it by."

"Against what?"

The carter thought about it with real care, and the mules stood, and the river went past.

"The other one going," he said.

Brom made a sound into his sleeve. Lira looked at the boots, and at the road going away south between its alders, six days of it, and at her own bare foot with the linen crossed over it in a figure of eight.

"Two marks," she said. "Karis."

"I heard." The purse came down over the cart's side in a small pale hand. "I shall want it understood that this is the board's money and it has bought a boot and a half's worth of leather that nobody can wear. In hand, a hundred and fourteen."

Lira packed the right one. It took the whole of a spare shirt, torn in strips and wound round her taped foot until the foot was the size of the boot. When she stood up in it she was two fingers taller on that side than on the other. She walked a few steps, and stopped, and walked a few more.

"It's like wearing a bucket," she said. "I can't feel the ground through it at all."

"Can you feel where your foot is?" said Cael.

She tried it again, and her face changed a little.

"Yes. That's her tape. I'd know where it was in a barrel." She tied the left boot to the cart's tail by its laces, where it hung all the way to Lowmarch, turning slowly, like a sign over a shop. "Don't anybody tell me what it looks like."

---

By noon of the second day he hated the tape, as he had been told that he would.

He could have borne the pain of it; he had carried pain before, on worse terms, and priced it and gone on. What he could not bear was that the thing was always right. He would reach for the water-skin, or turn his head to see what Lira had laughed at, or simply draw the breath that a hill wanted; and five bands of linen would close across his side a hair before the bone did, and tell him *there*. Every time, when he stopped to check it, the tape had known and he had not. A person can forgive an instrument for being wrong. He was learning that it was a good deal harder to forgive one for being right four thousand times a day.

There was no arguing with it, and he could not bear a thing that he could neither argue with nor measure, so he measured it.

A breath went in to a count of three before the tape spoke. On a level road he could take that breath at every fourth stride and never touch the edge. On a rise it wanted every third, and on the steepest of the valley's little hills every second, and there the breaths came so shallow and so quick that his head swam. He had always walked in fits. He had gone quick and then slow since he was a child on the lanes of Denvash, running to look at a thing and standing still to count it. He had watched a healer come down a mountain for a day and a half at one unbroken beat, and he had not been able to copy her for a hundred yards.

He tried it now beside the near mule, since there was nothing else to do and six days in which to do it.

The mule did not hurry and did not stop. Its near fore came down, and he put his left foot down with it; four of those, and a breath, to the count of three; four more. Whenever he ran ahead of the beat the tape caught him, and whenever he fell behind it the cart's wheel came up at his heel. Between those two there was a place the width of a plank where neither of them touched him, and by the afternoon of the eighty-sixth day he could stay on the plank for a mile together.

It was a small thing to be proud of. He entered it all the same. In two years of other people's fights and eighty-six days of road, nobody had ever taught him anything that he had so little wished to learn, and it had been taught him by a strip of pitch and a mule.

Lira walked at the off wheel, on the other side of the cart, and she counted nothing at all.

He knew, since he could hear her. She had been told a month, and it was plain that she had taken the month as she had taken the mule, as a thing agreed to and not to be discussed. She set the great boot down on its whole sole at every step and looked at the country. There was a good deal of it to see. The road went south down a broad valley of alder and rough pasture, with the grey mountain shrinking behind their left shoulders, and twice a day it forded some small brown water where the mules drank below the crossing. She named nothing and timed nothing. Once, in the middle of the second afternoon, she said across the cart that she had never before walked anywhere slowly enough to see what was growing on a wall, and that it was mostly moss.

Karis slept. She woke to eat, and to ask which day it was and write the number down, and then she folded herself up again between two boxes with Seln's coat over her and was gone. Cael watched her do it and was easier each time. On the mountain she had looked at her own hands before she looked at anything else, and by the third morning on this road she had stopped.

Seln found the sleeping places, and gave no report of how.

It was Brom that Cael watched on the eighty-seventh day, from the first light.

He walked behind the cart with his hands hanging, as he had walked behind another cart up a saddle, and every little while he turned his left wrist over and looked at the cuff. He ate his bread with the right hand. When the carter's off mule cast a shoe-nail in the middle of the morning and stood on three legs, it was Seln who held the hoof, and Brom stood by with both arms folded so tightly that the knuckles showed.

At the noon halt he cut a hazel rod from the roadside, a yard long and as straight as he could find, and pushed it upright into the turf of the halting-ground. Then he sat down on a chock-stone in front of it.

"What's that for?" said Lira.

"The middle of the day."

"You can see the middle of the day. It's up there."

"I can see near enough. She didn't say near enough." He had his eyes on the rod's shadow, which lay along the grass to the north, as long as his hand and growing shorter. "She said a week from the middle of the eightieth. I was there. I saw where the rock's shadow lay when she took her hands off it."

So they ate, and the shadow drew in. Nobody spoke of it, but Cael noticed that nobody strayed far either. The carter chewed, and looked from the rod to the big man and back, with the mild puzzlement of a person watching the customs of a foreign hold.

It took the better part of a quarter of an hour. The shadow came in to the width of three fingers and stood there, and stood, and then, by a hair that Cael would not have sworn to, it was on the other side of the rod.

"That's the week," said Brom.

He untied the cuff with his right hand and his teeth. Under it the seam was pale and dry, from the heel of the hand to the middle of the forearm. He bent the wrist down and brought it back. Then he folded the linen in four and put it inside his shirt, on the left, where he kept a dead slip with six words on it.

"Well?" said Lira. "Aren't you going to lift something? You've been looking at that cart for three days like a dog at a door."

"There's nothing wants lifting."

"There's three boxes."

"They're on a bed, where they ought to be. I'd only be taking one down to put it up again." He stood, and pulled the rod out of the ground, and laid it across his knee as though to break it; then he seemed to think better of that and slid it under the cart's canvas. "She gave me a week, and I've kept it to the hour. I'll not spend the first minute of the next one showing off."

Cael had half risen to say something, and he sat down again. He had been on the point of telling Brom that the week was out, and that he was free of it. He saw now that it would have been like telling a man the price of something he had made himself. Nobody had kept that week but Brom. He could finish it in whatever way he pleased.

---

The moss came on the fourth day, and the carter stopped his mules at the edge of it without being asked.

The valley had flattened and gone wet. For two hundred yards and more in front of them there was no pasture and no alder, only black water standing in sheets among yellow reed, with tussocks between as big as kneeling sheep; and across the whole of it somebody had laid a road. It was made of logs. They lay side by side, crosswise, each as thick as a man's leg and twice as long as the cart was wide, bedded on brushwood and sunk to half their depth. They ran away over the moss in a grey ribbed strip, like the backbone of something very long.

"How old is that?" said Karis. She had sat up.

The carter lifted one shoulder. "My father's."

"And when was it last mended?"

He lifted the other.

Brom had gone forward onto the first of the logs. He stood on it, and on the next, and then he came to one that was darker than its neighbours and put his heel down hard, and the heel went in. He drew it out with a sucking noise. The wood under the bark was the colour of wet bread.

"One in ten," he said. "Maybe one in six. They've gone soft from the middle, where you can't see it, and they'll bear a man. I'd not put a loaded axle on them." He looked back at the carter. "What did you weigh, coming this way last time?"

"Never came. He did."

"Then we'll do it as your father would have, if he'd any sense. She goes over light. The load walks."

It was the fan again, Cael thought, and he was glad to see that it was the fan made small; there was no slope in it and nothing overhead. But somebody had to learn which logs were sound before a wheel was trusted to any of them, and there was only one way that the five of them had ever done that.

Lira had the same thought. He saw it come over her face, and he saw her set it against a healer's month.

"Walking," she said. "I'm allowed to walk. She said it'd walk in a week as well as the other, and that was eight days ago."

"She said no running for a month," said Brom.

"I know what she said; I was under her hands when she said it. This is a flat road, at a walk, in a boot the size of a trough. Somebody mark them behind me."

She went out along the logs. She did not run them, and Cael, who had watched her run sixty-two hundreds of a mountain, found it stranger to see her walk these. She set the packed boot down in the middle of each log and leaned on it, and waited, with her arms a little out from her sides; and at every one that gave she said "Soft," without turning her head, and stepped over. Seln followed three logs behind with a lump of chalk that the carter kept for his tallies, and drew a cross.

It took her the better part of an hour to do two hundred yards, and there were a hundred and forty crosses.

Then they emptied the cart. The mules were led over one at a time, picking their way between the chalk marks with their ears laid back. After them the cart itself went across with her bed bare, the carter at her head, and Seln and Cael each at a wheel to turn it by the spokes whenever the rim came up to a cross. He could do that much. It was done at the height of his hip and it asked nothing of his ribs that the tape would not allow.

The boxes stood on the last of the dry ground, in their order.

Brom looked at them. He had stood so in front of a cart in a wash once, Cael remembered, and in front of two hundred and sixty yards of scree, working out a weight. He was not working anything out now. He walked up to the middle box, with the corner that was worn white, and bent his knees, and took hold of it by its two rope beckets, one in each hand.

He did not swing it to his back. He stood up with it in front of him, held against his chest in both arms, as a man carries a thing through a doorway; and for a breath he only stood there.

His left hand had been in a sling and a cuff, by turns, since a river on the fifty-third day. That made thirty-five days. Cael watched the two wrists, as he had watched them under a cart on the eighth narrowing, and no grey started at either. There was a hundredweight of copied paper on them and they were the colour of wrists.

"It's not heavy," said Brom, to nobody. He sounded a little surprised. "I'd thought it would be heavier."

He carried it the whole way over. He came back for the left-hand one, and then for the right; and when Seln stepped forward for the third, since three crossings of that moss was a long way under a box, Brom shook his head and took that too. The carter watched him go by each time with a hundredweight in his arms and a look of deep content on his plain face, and plainly could not make out what he was seeing. Carrying boxes was only what a big man was for.

"He's pleased about something," the carter said at last, to Cael. It was the longest speech of the six days.

"He's been waiting eight mornings to pick that up."

"It's a box."

"Yes," said Cael. "It is."

---

They made their last camp on the evening of the eighty-ninth day, on a halting-ground at the top of a long hill, with a river shining below them in the dusk.

It was Lowmarch's river. He knew its colour, and the way it bent. They had come back to it from the west, by a bank that he had never stood on; and somewhere down there in the dark was a round thatched shed with a drum in it, and a horse walking a circle that had once drawn their cart across a province. Seln had a fire going with his flint. The carter sat at it as a matter of course, and held his enormous feet to the warmth.

Karis was awake. She had been awake since noon, sitting up between the boxes with her notebook on her knee, and she had the colour back in her face that six days of sleep could give it.

"Tomorrow is the ninetieth," she said. "The plank, and a smith. I have a slip in my coat with a horned head on it, and I want to hear what he does with a job that ended in a no." She turned a leaf. "And Thornwater on the ninety-third. That's four days, and two of them are road."

"We haven't said we're going to Thornwater," said Lira.

"She said nine days, and he said nine days back to her. I was there; I had my eyes shut, but I was there."

"He didn't say we'd come. He said the number."

"With him it's the same thing."

Lira looked across the fire at Cael, and he kept his eyes on the book.

"You've been reading that crew the whole way down," she said. "At every halt. I've watched you go round the stones."

"They don't light a fire."

"What, never?"

"Five halting-grounds, and not a new ash on one of them. The chock-stones are where they found them. They eat cold, and they sleep in a row with the mules at the windward end, and in the morning there's nothing to show for it but where the grass lay down." He turned a page back to look at what he had put. "We've had a fire every night since the ford, on the strength of a finding. He's had none for longer than I've known what a hold was, on the strength of never having needed one."

"That's not better," said Lira. "That's only older."

"I didn't say it was better. I said that I could read it."

Brom had the carter's pot between his knees, since their own kettle was at the bottom of a gorge, and he was scouring it with a handful of grass, slowly, with his lips moving. Cael knew that look as well. He was getting two speeches ready, one for a ferryman and one for a smith, and he was putting the worse of them first.

Cael had the day's lines to put down, and they were short ones.

*Eighty-ninth day. The west bank, a morning short of the ferry. The boxes rode from Oxhollow on the keeper's grain cart, for nothing, since the map said no. In hand, a hundred and fourteen; a boot. Six owed to the smith and a rope to the ferryman.*

*Mine: I breathe to the tape and walk to a mule. Brom: the week kept, to the hour, and three boxes carried over a moss. Lira walks. Karis is awake.*

*A crew went down this road a day ahead of us and never left a mark out of its line. I read them at every halt. They'll have been at the plank tonight.*

*The nine: held. Deployment since the Line: one.*

He blotted it. Down in the valley a single light had come out, low, by the water, and he thought that it was most likely the lower inn.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-24.md

# Chapter 24 — Under the Stone

The ferryman would not take silver for his rope.

He said so on the western landing, with the scow rocking at his back and the far drum already groaning. He was the same long sour man who liked animals and nobody else, and he had stood with his arms folded through the whole of Brom's account: the wash, and the well, and a peeled log on a hill of plates, and at the last a gorge with a cart in it. Brom told it as he would have told a debt, in order, leaving nothing out.

"So it's under a mountain," said the ferryman.

"With a cart on top of it. I'd sooner say so here than have you find it out from the wall."

"Sixty yards of four-strand. I laid that rope up myself, in the cold term, with him turning the hook." He jerked his chin at his boy. Then he looked at Karis, who had the purse out on her knee, and his long face soured further. "Put that away. What'd I do with it? There's not a rope-walk nearer than the turf fire, and I can't draw a wagon out of a river with marks."

"Then what do you want for it?" said Lira.

"A rope. When you've one. I'll not put a figure on it, for a figure's a thing you can pay and walk off from."

He turned his back on them and clicked his tongue at the far bank.

Karis wrote it down as he had said it. *Owed: one rope, sixty yards, four-strand, to the ferryman. No price.* She put the purse away uncounted, and Cael thought that it was the first debt she had ever entered without a number beside it, and that she did not like the look of the line.

They went over with the Oxhollow cart, a copper a wheel and a copper a beast out of the carter's own bag. Halfway across Brom lifted his head. In the round shed on the eastern bank something brown was walking its circle, steadily, with one white foot coming round and round into the light, and Brom watched that foot for the rest of the crossing with the face of a man who has seen a child of his doing well at a trade.

The meadow had thinned, for the cold had sent most of the tents home, and there were five fires where there had been a dozen; but the long eave was where it had always been, and under it twelve feet of tarred oak, and behind the oak a bald brown head bent over a book.

Pike saw them from the landing road. He looked at a strange cart with three boxes on it, and at a girl in one boot of her own and one the size of a bucket, and at how Cael held his left arm; and then he looked at all of it a second time, as he did.

"You're down," he said.

"We're down."

"You'd a cart when you went up."

"It's in a gorge," said Brom.

"So I'd heard, from a man who came in last night." Pike set his pen in its pot. "I'll say your line when I've read your paper. Read mine first."

Lira was already reading. She had gone to the left-hand post, from habit, and she was standing in front of two slips that had not been there on the seventieth morning. One was bark and one was a shingle, and on each of them, under a hold's mark and a line of words, stood a word in the square black hand, in capitals, which Cael had seen on this plank only once before.

*HOLD.*

It had had one name after it, that time. Under each of these there were five, set down in the order in which they had been spoken at the plank on the ninth day, and his own was the last.

"That's us," said Lira. Her voice had gone up. "That's our names. Who asked you to keep these?"

"Holds came to the ferry. Asked who. I said you."

Pike had not raised his head, and he said it level, as he would have told her the price of standing-money.

"Asked who what?"

"Who they ought to have. One's a bull wants bringing down off the top grazing before the snow shuts it, and he's killed a dog. The other's a ford that wants staking. They'd each of them heard a line said in this meadow about a river and five dead hounds." He blotted something. "I told them you were six days off on a hill. They said they'd wait a fortnight. After that they'll have Teague."

Cael stood and looked at his own name in another man's capitals.

He had been written on a good many boards. A woman in Ardenmere had chalked him up with a question mark, and a panel had posted him at a rate that it had scattered on purpose, and for two years a house had put his name on a card every week because a schedule told it to. Nobody had ever before gone to the keeper of a book and asked who was good, and been given him. He had stood in an inn, forty days ago, and heard Pike say that he could not send a hold a crew that had never yet had anything go wrong. Something had gone wrong for them twice since then, and they had come back from it both times, and here it was on a shingle.

"A fortnight," said Lira. "They'd wait a fortnight, for us, with a bull loose." She looked round at the others, and her eyes were very bright. "He didn't say fast. Nobody said fast. They said *who*."

---

Karis had the paper out of her coat.

It had come down a mountain inside her shirt and six days along a valley, and it was soft at the folds. Pike laid it on the oak and put two fingers on it, and read it to himself from the careful writing at the head to the foot, where five strokes stood side by side over a horned head and three wavy lines; and then he read it again.

"He's marked it," he said. "And paid you there, by the paper."

"Seventy," said Karis. "In silver, counted onto his own board."

"And the map?"

"He has it. He followed it with his finger from end to end, and then he said that his carts would go round, and went to his hearth for the bag."

Pike nodded slowly. He opened the book at the leaf with five names, and set the flat stone on its upper corner, and turned his shoulder to the meadow. There were perhaps thirty people within hearing, and Cael saw the hide-woman among them, with her knife on her thigh, and under the ash an old carter who lifted his chin.

"High traverse, Oxhollow to Thornwater. Mapped. Delivered at the ford. Carts go round."

He stopped there, with his mouth a little open, as a man stops on a stair to feel for the next tread. Cael knew what the next one was, for he had heard it on a river bank at dusk, from a man with a band of leather on his wrist.

"Cart lost. Crew of five, three hurt, none lost. Seventy."

It went down the meadow in that level carrying voice, past five fires. A woman looked at Lira's boot. A man by the nearest tent said something to his neighbour, low, and the neighbour looked at the strange cart, and then at the empty place beside the smith's shed where a hired one had stood. The Oxhollow carter had halted his mules at the edge of the grass to listen. At *carts go round* he had nodded slowly, like a person who hears his own errand read out to strangers; and at *cart lost* he looked at his boots.

"Is it wrong?" said Pike.

He put it to all five, as he put it to anybody. Cael went through the line a word at a time, for the place where he could say so. *Mapped* was true, and *delivered*; carts would go round, for an old man with a tally-stick had said it in those words. There had been a cart, and there was none, and three of them had been hurt. He looked for the thing that the line left out, and found it at once, and it was everything: a ledge that had shown no crack, and a page marked derived, and eight seconds of fire. None of that was in Pike's column, and he had known since a river bank that it would not be.

"No," he said.

Pike wrote, and the pen went to the horn pot and came back four times. When the line was on the page he did a thing that Cael had not seen him do before; he dropped the nib to the space beneath it and wrote four words more, smaller, and drew a short rule under them.

*less six, the cart.*

"Seventy's what the ford paid. Sixty-four's what the job was worth. That's what goes on the wall."

"Fair."

It was Brom who said it. He had come up to the oak, with his two hands hanging open, and he was looking at the small words under the line as he had looked at a smith's cut axle.

"It was his cart, and we brought him back a story about it. I'd have written it the same."

Pike straightened, and gave the whole of it to the meadow a second time, from the first word to the last, with the six taken off at the end; and this time nobody along the fires murmured. He did not go to the iron box, since there was nothing in it of theirs. He drove the soft paper down onto the spike beneath the plank, among the dead slips, and that was the end of a mountain.

Cael found that he did not mind it.

He turned that over, since he had expected to. On the bank at Oxhollow he had written that he kept waiting to mind, and he had gone on waiting for six days of road. The line said that they had lost a cart, and it said so to thirty strangers, twice. And he stood under an eave with five bands of linen round his chest and felt only that a thing had been counted, by a person who counted everything alike; it was the seventh line under their names, and it was worth sixty-four, and both of those were so.

Karis had the purse in her hand, and she had not opened it.

"I want one thing straight, for my own page," she said. "The seventy is in here. I counted it at a ford, and it hasn't altered on the road. Earned stands at a hundred and thirty-two. What you have written under that line is another figure, and I don't believe that it is money."

"It's what I'd tell a hold you were worth for that job, if a hold came and asked me," said Pike. "The six you'll count out to a smith yourselves, before dark, if I know you. I only take it off where the meadow can see it go."

"Seven," said Lira, under her breath. She was counting on her fingers. "A boat, a fence, a wagon, a mile behind a mule. A ford. A well. A mountain."

"And a bull, if we want it," said Brom.

---

It was while the pen was back in its pot that Cael saw the paper.

He had been reading the plank, as he read it every time, from the left-hand post to the right, and there were nine slips besides the two with their names on. He had sorted them and found nothing among them that weighed much, and his eye had come to rest, for want of anything better, on Pike's flat stone.

It was a grey river stone the size of a loaf, and it lay on the upper corner of the open book, as it always lay. Something was under its far edge: a corner showed there, toward Pike's side of the oak, no wider than three fingers, and it was neither bark nor hide. It was paper, of a good cream weight, such as a clerk writes on at a desk.

There was a curve of dust across the corner, and he looked at that for a breath before he understood it. The dust followed the rim of the stone exactly, a fine pale line, as a tide leaves its line on a beach; so the paper had not been slid there this morning. It had lain under that stone, in that one place, for long enough to take the stone's shape.

Every other slip that Pike had was on a tack, and he read them all out to anybody who was frying dough.

Cael leaned a little over the oak. The writing ran away from him, toward the man with the book, and he read it upside down, as he had once read a bailiff's list on a counter in Denvash.

*Recovery. Surveyor's instruments abandoned at a site on the Fallow road. East of Thornwater; the second day; where the road leaves its line at the rise and comes back to it. One hundred marks (one hundred).*

There was no hold's mark at its foot. There was a seal: a hook and a bar, in dark wax.

He read it a second time, to be sure of the figure. Then he reached across the oak and drew the paper out from under the stone by its corner, and the dust-line broke. He turned the sheet round and held it out to Pike, in his fingers, saying nothing, as any crew holds out a slip that it wishes to hear said.

Pike's face stopped.

Cael had no other word for it. He had watched that face for eighty days, and it was never still; it looked at a thing and then looked again, and something in it went on working like a mill between the two looks. Now the one arm lay on the book and the brown eyes rested on a sheet of cream paper, and nothing in the face moved at all.

He counted, since he could no more have helped it than breathing, and it was nine before the man spoke.

"That's the Fallow Ring."

He said it to the paper. Lira had turned round from her two slips, and Brom from the spike. None of them answered him, since the name meant nothing to any of the five, and Pike raised his eyes and saw that it meant nothing.

"That's quiet ground."

Beside Cael's shoulder a notebook shut with a small clap.

He did not need to look, for he knew whose it was, and he knew what was folded in the middle box, forty yards off, with two lines ruled under two of its words. Karis had heard those two words once before, dropped by a woman with a sack of barley on her hip, and she had asked nobody what they meant for thirty-two days.

"I have that noun," she said. Her voice was very careful. "I have had it since the fifty-eighth day, from a carter's wife, in three sentences, and I have had nobody to put a question to. You have just said it to me yourself, at your own plank. I think that I may ask now. What is it?"

Pike laid the cream sheet down on the oak, face upward, and put his hand on it.

"Quiet ground's where the sigil goes to sleep. Yours. Everyone's. You walk a road, and there's a place on it where your Arbiter stops — not slows; stops — and you walk on past and it's back. There's a handful of them. Along the void roads, all of them. Carters walk round them, and have as long as there've been carters. The dogs live in them. Stillhounds. Nobody's asked them why. Why any of it, nobody knows and nobody's needed to, because the rule's *don't go in*, and the rule's never once failed anybody who kept it."

It was the longest thing that Cael had ever heard him say, and he had said it once. He let it stand, without going back over a word of it, and that was stranger than what was in it.

"That's what a carter knows. That's what I know. There's not a man on the ferry knows a word more."

Under the eave there was no sound, and out in the meadow a mule shook its harness.

Cael had heard *yours* and *everyone's*, and he had thought of a place behind his own breastbone, where a small bright thing had gathered once on a morning in a windowless room, and looked at him for eleven seconds, and gone out. It had been dark ever since, and he kept that thought where it was.

"You've seen it?" said Lira. "This ring."

"I've walked past the Fallow Ring two hundred times. Never once stepped over the line. Thirty years on the roads and I've never met a practitioner who did it twice."

"What's the line?"

It was Karis, and it was the second of the four questions that she had swallowed in a cart-yard.

"You'll know," he said. "Your Arbiter tells you. It stops."

---

Karis went to the cart.

She walked the forty yards without hurrying, and climbed the wheel, and had the lid off the middle box; and when she came back she had a folded leaf in one hand and a roll of calfskin under her arm. She laid the leaf on the oak beside the cream paper. It was in Cael's own writing, three sentences with a question mark after the first, and under two of the words there were two ruled lines.

Then she unrolled the hide, and held it open with her wrists, and put one finger down.

Cael knew the place. East of a square with a dot in it, two days of ticks out, a double line left its straight and went out to the left in a curve and came back to itself, as a path goes round a pond; and inside the curve a carter had drawn nothing.

She read the paper's third sentence over to herself, with her lips, and her finger had not moved.

"He has drawn that. A road that leaves its line and comes back. He drew it for his own wheels, and never drew what was in the middle, since he had no business there. And she said that everybody knew it, and that nobody went in." She took the finger away. "I have had those two for a month and could do nothing with either. This is the third, and it has a price on it."

She looked up at Pike.

"A location. And a contract."

"It's a paper with a hundred on it," said Pike. "I'd not call it more."

Cael looked at the three things lying side by side on the tarred oak. There was a leaf in his own hand that held a stranger's passing word, and a calfskin that a carter had drawn for his wheels over twenty-two years, and a cream sheet under a seal that he had first seen burned into the end of a cask. None of them had been meant for the others' company. The woman with the barley had never looked at her husband's map with a pencil in her hand, and the carter had certainly never read a broker's paper; and yet all three were speaking of one patch of grass, two days beyond a fence, as though they had agreed on it beforehand.

"Why's it under your stone?" said Lira. "Everything else you've got is on a tack. You shout it at people."

"I said it. I'm bound to say it; twice, the day it came, to the whole grass, with the price." He turned the cream sheet a little with one finger. "The broker's clerk brought it up from the landing the morning after the last sack, sealed as you see it, with a hundred marks in a bag, counted. I've had that seal on bales for thirty years. I never had it before on a slip that sends a crew two days past Thornwater."

"And nobody took it."

"Nobody scratched. There were forty in this meadow that day, and when I'd done you could have heard the river. So I put the stone on it. A thing on a tack is a thing I'm offering."

He said that last quite mildly, and Cael understood that he had been told something about the man. Pike wrote down whatever was put in front of him and read it to anyone; but he had found, in thirty years, one paper that he would not hang up between two fences and a wheel, and he had kept it where a person must go looking.

Cael had gone looking, and he weighed that, with the oak under his hands.

A hundred marks was more than everything that the board had paid them in ninety days, the mountain included. It was to walk two days east of a hold where they were known, and pick up something that somebody had left lying, and carry it home. He could find no opponent in it. There was a ring on a road, and a rule with no reason given, and instruments, which were only things; and he heard himself thinking that, and distrusted it, since he had thought the same about a shelf.

"What's a surveyor want out there?" said Brom. "On ground a carter won't put a wheel on."

"That I couldn't tell you," said Pike. "It's not a trade I've ever seen come over my ferry."

"Then who left the things?"

"The paper doesn't say. I've read it more than twice."

Seln had not spoken at all. He stood at the plank's end with his hands behind him, and he had not yet troubled with the words; it was the sheet itself that he studied, where it lay, as a joiner studies a board.

"I'll not mark it tonight," said Pike. "That's not a thing I'd say of a fence. Take it in with you, and put it on your boards, and look at it with a lamp. If you bring it me in the morning I'll say it and write your strokes." He pushed the paper across the oak with one finger, until it lay in front of Cael. "And if you don't, it goes back where it was."

---

They paid the smith before the light went.

He was at his door, as he had been on the seventy-first morning, and he looked past them at the place under his lean-to where a cart with a cut axle ought to have been standing. Brom told it again, and it was shorter this time, since a smith needed no telling what loose stone did to a wheel.

"Floated," the smith said, when it was done.

"To the edge, and over, with her shafts up."

"I'd cut that axle true."

"You had. She never once ran crooked. It wasn't the cart." Brom was quiet a little. "You kept my seat board, and I'm sorry for it."

The smith held out his hand, and Karis counted six thick coins into it, and he shut his fist on them without looking down.

"A hundred and eight," she said.

It was done. Cael had carried that six since the seventy-first morning, as one carries a stone in a boot, and he waited to feel lighter and did not. The smith had gone back into his shed already, and presently the rasp began again. A cart was only a cart, and the man had been paid for it twice over; all the same there would be a clean place under that lean-to for a good while, and Brom stood looking at it with his hands hanging until Karis took him by the sleeve.

The account was square, as far as coin could square it. There remained a rope with no figure against it, and a line in a book that said *cart lost*, and neither of those could be paid off; they could only be added to, by whatever the five of them did next.

He came on Lira at the roof's edge.

She was standing where the eave's shadow stopped, with her toes on the line of it, her own boot and the great one side by side, and she was looking east. The saddle road went up there, pale in the last light, toward a wash, and a ford, and a fence; and beyond the fence there was a road that none of them had walked, with a bend in it. The cold was coming up off the river behind them. Along the meadow the fires had drawn in to red, and somebody was calling a child home.

He stood beside her. The others had gone in, and through the open door he could hear a boy being told to hold a cloth taut.

"Two hundred times. He counted."

He had not meant to say it aloud. It had been going round in him since the plank, a thing that he could not sort. A man had walked a road for thirty years, and each time that he came to a certain place he had gone round, and each time he had made a mark in his head. He had never once put his foot over, and he knew the number of his own refusals to the unit.

Lira did not turn her head.

"So do you. I've never once heard you say never."

He looked at her.

"He counts what he hasn't done," she said. "That's all that is. Two hundred times he didn't. You'd have gone round it the first time, and the second, and you'd have written both down. And the third time you'd have stood there with your book shut, working out what it would cost to look." She scuffed the great boot along the shadow's edge. "I've watched you price things since a lane in Ardenmere. You've said *not yet* to me, and *not here*, and *no* a hundred times. You don't say *never*. I don't think you know how."

It was true, and he had not known it, and she had. He had refused nine things a stride at a time for ninety days, and thought himself a careful person; but a refusal that is made again at every stride is the opposite of a rule, for it is a door that somebody is holding shut with his hand.

"You want to see it," said Lira.

"Yes."

"So do I." She said it low, and she was still looking at the road. "He said your Arbiter tells you. It stops. I've spent three months not doing the one thing I'm good at, on purpose, a stride at a time, and it went off anyway when a ledge fell. I'd like to stand somewhere that I couldn't. I'd like to know what's left when it isn't me doing the *not*."

He thought of a question that stood in his book under the fifty-ninth day, in her words, with nothing written beneath it. She had asked what she was becoming if nobody was measuring, and he had not answered, and nobody had. He did not suppose that a ring on a road would answer it either. But it would tell her one thing plainly, at one stride, without a sheet or a panel or a coach; and he saw that she had heard that in the old man's three short sentences before any of the rest of them.

Inside, a voice had begun to count lines.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-25.md

# Chapter 25 — Tenth

Pike wrote the wall out again that evening, from the top, on a clean length of sailcloth, and Lira counted the lines as they went up.

The room was warmer than Cael remembered it, and emptier. The cold term had sent half the crews home to their own fences, and the benches down both sides had room on them; but there was a fire in the great hearth that a man could have stood in, and it threw its light across the plaster where the old sheet had hung since the sixtieth day. A boy held the new cloth taut with both palms. The one hand moved over it, steadily, and went to the horn pot on the bench and came back.

*Teague.* It stood by itself on the first line, as it always had.

"Two," said Lira. "Three."

She was not sitting. She stood in the middle of the floor with her arms folded and her weight on her own boot, and her lips moved at each name. The five of them had the boards nearest the door, where they had sat on the forty-ninth night, and on the boards between Brom's elbows lay a sheet of cream paper with a lamp beside it.

"Eight. Nine."

The pen went to the pot. Pike wrote five names on one line, in the order in which they had been spoken at a plank, and the boy shifted his hands down the cloth.

"Tenth," said Lira.

She said it quite quietly. Two lines that had stood over theirs since the day they came now stood under them, and the shingle pair were at the foot still. Cael looked for what the rung was made of, since he had learned in this room that it was always made of something. It was a mountain, less a cart. The wall had no place to say so. It said only that a hold which asked Pike who ought to walk a bad hill would now be given five names before it was given two others.

"We lost a cart and went up two," said Brom slowly. He had the jug, and he had not yet poured. "I'd not have reckoned it so."

"You'd have reckoned the cart," said Karis. "He reckoned the map as well. It's the same sum; you stopped halfway."

"Which two have we gone over?" Lira had come back to the boards at last. "I can't tell from here. Half of them are trades."

"Then go and read it," said Pike, from the hearth, without turning his head. "I don't call out who's gone down. I write who's where."

She went and read it, with her nose a hand's breadth from the wet ink, and came back looking thoughtful. One of the two was a crew of four who had brought a string of mules through the cold road's pass and lost none of them. She had seen them in the meadow, she said, and they were none of them young, and one had a wooden foot.

"So that's what tenth is," she said. "It's just in front of a man with a wooden foot who never lost a mule."

"I'd not sneer at him," said Brom. "I've lost a cart."

"I wasn't sneering. I was working out how good you'd have to be to get past him by two."

Pike had sat down at last, on the hearth bench, under his own handwriting, and somebody had put a cup into his hand. Cael took his own cup and went over, as he had gone over once before to ask about a ledger-keeper in Ardenmere.

"The man at the top," he said. "You told us at the plank that he came in last night."

Pike drank, and looked at the fire, and then he looked across the room at the boards by the door, where a lamp stood beside a cream sheet.

[[TIER-B: Pike, in the board's room, of Teague — "Teague's nine up… He read that slip yesterday. Read it twice… I'd have done the same, and I told him, and he said he knew." — coordinator to supply protected text]]

Cael stood with the cup in his hand.

He had read that man's feet in the silt of a ford, and his halts at five halting-grounds after it. He had watched him count three people on a sill before he looked at a room, and had been told by him on a river bank, in a level voice, what a mountain was worth. And the same man had stood at that plank a night ago, with a hundred marks under his hand, and had read it twice, and put it back under a stone.

It was a reason against. He entered it as one, with the name of the man who had given it, and he noticed that Pike had not told him what the reason was. He did not think that Pike knew.

---

It had been the evening before, the eighty-ninth, with the five still a camp short of the river, when Teague counted his three off the ferry.

He did it on the eastern landing, with the scow's rail still under his hand. The spear was ashore already, and grounded. The boy had the two mules by one rein and was looking up the bank at the lower inn's chimney, where there would be something in a pot. The bow came off last, and stepped down onto the planks with the right foot, and stood on it.

Three, and whole. That hip had had six days of road since a healer told him the price of it, and the bow had not once asked to shorten a stage.

He sent the boy to the smith's with the mules and went up to the plank by himself, as he always did, before he had so much as washed. Pike was taking in his book for the night. Teague read the oak from the left-hand post, a slip at a time, and at the second slip he stopped.

*HOLD.* It was in the square capitals, and it was not his. There were five names under it, and five again under the same word on the shingle beside.

He stood in front of them for as long as it took to be sure what he felt. He had asked for that word once, on a ford, and had it struck with one line while he was on a ledge with a string of salt; and he had never grudged the striking, since a hold cannot wait on a captain's convenience. Now two holds had come down to a ferry and asked a one-armed man who they ought to have. That was the board doing its work. If he minded it, he would have to mind arithmetic.

He went on along the oak. There was nothing else on it that weighed, and he was turning away when he saw the stone.

Pike's stone lay on the book's corner, where it had lain for as long as Teague had been coming to that plank; and under its far edge there was the corner of a sheet of paper. A line of dust lay across the corner, fine and pale, drawn along the stone's rim. He had been four weeks up the north water, and that sheet had plainly lain there for most of them. A waybill does not gather dust under a stone.

"What's that?"

Pike's eyes went to the stone and came back, twice.

"Paper," he said. "It's been said. You weren't here."

"Then I'll read it."

He was given it, as he had been given a struck slip on the fifty-seventh day. It was good paper, heavier than anything the holds wrote on. He read it from the first word to the seal, and then he began again at the top, more slowly.

He priced it as he would have priced a stage, a thing at a time.

The price came first, and it was a hundred. That was too much. A surveyor's kit was a thing that one man carried on a strap; he had seen such a kit once, years ago and a long way west. It was two days out to that rise and two home, on a road he knew, and nobody paid a hundred marks for four days and a case. But *too much* was only a reason if a man could say what it was too much for, and he could not.

The hirer was the broker, by the wax. Teague had carried that man's bales. He paid to the day, and argued every copper beforehand and none after; and he had never in his life sent a crew farther than his own landing. That was odd. It was not wrong. A broker posts for whoever puts money in his hand.

The ground he knew. He had been round the Fallow Ring eleven times, with carts, by the ruts, at noon, and had looked across the grass each time at a row of grey stumps and kept walking. Every carter's child knew what was said of it. He had never put it to the proof, and he did not need a paper to tell him why not: he had three behind him, and in that place the spear would be a man with a spear, and the bow a man with a bad hip, and the boy a boy. He would not take them in for a hundred, or for twice a hundred.

That was a reason not to take it. It was a good one, and it was his, and it would have done for any captain on the sheet.

But it was not what had stopped him at the second reading. Something in the paper itself was wrong, as a load is wrong on a mule a mile before the girth shows it. He went round it twice more, looking for the place. The witness was not named, and there was nobody at the far end to say that the things had ever lain there. The words were a clerk's. None of that was a reason either. He had taken worse paper than this from better men, and brought it home.

He could not find it. He knew only that he would not have liked to see one of his own carrying that sheet up the east road, and that he could not have told them why.

He laid it back under the stone himself. He set the corner on its own line of dust, as nearly as he could, and took his hand away.

"That's what I'd have done with it," said Pike.

"I know."

It was all either of them said. Pike shut the book and tucked it under what was left of his arm, and Teague stood a breath longer in front of two slips with five names on each.

They would be at the ferry tomorrow. He had kept a day ahead of them from Oxhollow without trying to, and he knew their pace, with a taped boy and a girl on one boot. The boy would read this plank as he had read a doorway, from one end to the other, leaving nothing out. He would find the stone.

Teague had said four things to him on a river bank, and every one of them had been counted before it was spoken. This was not a thing that could be counted. A captain did not walk up to another crew and hand it a feeling, as though a feeling were a figure; if he had, he would have been asked what it rested on, and rightly, and he would have had no answer but the hair on his own neck.

So he said nothing, and it sat poorly with him. He went up the bank to find the boy, and whatever was in the pot.

---

They held the council once, at their own boards, with the lamp turned up.

Cael had set the cream sheet in the middle of the table, where all five could reach it, and he gave the order as he would have given it for any contract: each of them to speak, and nobody to answer until the last had done. Brom poured. He had allowed one jug, on the strength of a line said at a plank, and had made it plain that the paper under the lamp had not yet earned a second.

"Karis," said Cael.

She had her notebook shut under both hands, and she did not open it.

"I shall be brief, since I am not disinterested, and I want that known before I begin." Her voice was small and exact. "I have three boxes on a cart outside. Every leaf in them is about the country we left, except the handful that I have written myself since a river. In ninety days I have met one thing on this side that somebody else thought worth a sheet of paper, and it is lying there."

She nodded at the lamp.

"He says that there is a place on a road where an Arbiter stops. He says it stops at a line, and that a person knows the line by her own. If that is so, then it can be found with nothing but feet, by anybody, and found again in the same place the next day by somebody else. I have been taught that an Arbiter cannot be tested at all; that it is the one thing every instrument we own takes for granted. I should like to stand where one fails." She drew a breath. "I would go for nothing. I say so in order that nobody should count my hand as a vote for the hundred."

Cael had known her for two years, and in all that time he had heard her ask for a room, and for silence, and once for an hour more with a restricted shelf. He had never before heard her say what she wanted in front of four people and then decline to argue for it. She sat with both hands on the shut notebook, a little flushed, and looked at nobody.

"Brom."

Brom had turned the paper toward himself with one finger, and he had been doing a sum. Cael knew the look of it. He had worn that look over a wash and a fan and a cut axle.

[[TIER-B: Brom, at the council — "A survey kit's a cart's worth… That's not the reason. That's what it is." — coordinator to supply protected text]]

He pushed the paper back to the middle of the table and picked up his cup.

"Lira."

She had sat down at last, sideways on the bench, with the great boot stuck out into the room.

"I've walked the east road four times and I know every stone of it as far as that fence. Past the fence it's two days more, on ruts that the grain carts use, to a hill. Then it's whatever a surveyor leaves behind him, picked up, and brought back the same way." She looked round at them. "I'm saying it's a walk and a carry. I'm not saying it isn't the other thing."

"The dogs live in them," said Brom.

"I heard him. I've been bitten by one; I've a foot to prove it." She shrugged, with some effort. "That's mine. A walk and a carry, and my eyes open."

They were looking at him now. He was the nearest thing that they had to a captain, and it was his to say last but one.

He had a good many reasons. He could have said what he had said to Lira under the eave, that he wished to see it, and it would have been true. He could have said that a hold had asked for them by name, and that a crew which was asked for ought to find out what it was made of. There was a rope with no figure against it. There was a cart in a gorge, and a line in a book.

All of those were his, and he saw that each of them was a way of dressing the plain thing so that it should look better on him. The plain thing was that five people had a hundred and eight marks and the cold coming, and that this paid as much again for four days of road.

"It pays."

Lira's eyes rested on him a breath longer than the words needed, and went back to the lamp. She did not say what else she knew.

"Seln."

---

Seln had not touched the sheet since it came in from the plank.

He drew it toward him now by one corner, and held it up to the lamp without reading a word of it, so that the flame stood behind the sheet, and he looked through the paper as a person looks through a window.

"I have listened to four cases that turn upon what this says," he said. "I should like to add what it is."

He laid it down, and touched the left-hand edge.

"There is a margin. It has been pricked, here and here, at even distances, with a wheel; that is done so that every sheet from a desk is ruled alike. No hold has such a wheel. The broker's clerk writes on whatever comes to hand." His finger moved to the head of the sheet. "*Recovery.* One word, set by itself, with a stop after it. That is how a class of business is named at the top of a paper that will afterward be put away with others of its class. A carter would have begun, *I have lost.*"

"And the sum," said Karis softly. She had seen it at the plank.

"And the sum. It is written in words, and then again, in a bracket. A person is taught to do that who has been warned that a figure may be altered and a word may not. I was so taught, at a ruled desk, when I was not a great deal older than he is." The mild eyes came up. "The place is given as a description. A day, and a rise, and a road that bends. Every carter in that meadow has a name for it, and our friend at the plank used the name at once. Whoever wrote this either did not know it or was not permitted to put it down."

He folded his hands.

"Somebody wrote this who learned to write inside."

"Inside the Line," said Brom.

"Inside the Line, at a desk, from a book of forms. I do not say whose desk. I say that it was not composed on that landing. It was copied there, or else it came over the water as it stands, with the money."

For some time the only sound was the fire. Cael looked at a cream sheet that had lain a month under a river stone, and saw it as he had not seen it at the plank: a thing with a ruled margin, lying on an inn's boards in a country that kept no paper. He had walked east for ninety days, and here was a piece of the west on his own table, with a seal on it that told him nothing.

"It goes in."

Seln said it mildly, to nobody in particular.

"Is that you telling us to leave it?" said Lira.

"That's not a reason not to go."

He gave them no more than that, and he sat back out of the lamplight, like a man who has handed in a report and does not mean to argue it.

Karis had opened the notebook after all. She was counting on the edge of a leaf, in small strokes.

"If we mark it in the morning, that is the ninety-first," she said. "The Oxhollow cart rests a day and goes east on the ninety-second, with our boxes, and we in front of it. That brings us to the fence on the afternoon of the ninety-third."

She stopped, and Cael saw the stroke of her pencil stop with her.

Nobody at the table needed the sum done twice. A square woman had stood in a gateway on the eighty-fourth day, with a mule's cheek-strap in her hand, and had said nine days. The road to the Fallow Ring went through Thornwater, and by no arrangement of theirs it went through on the day that she had named.

Brom was looking at his left wrist. Lira had her eyes on the great boot, and on what was taped inside it; she had asked for that ankle once, and been told what it was worth and to whom it was owed. Seln studied his cup. And Cael had stood by a well with a sentence ready, six days ago, and had found that he had no right to it.

Not one of the five said that a healer might be brought. He watched four people not say it, each for a reason of his own, and he was prouder of them than he could have told them.

Karis wrote. She turned the leaf so that the lamp fell on it, and they could all read what she had put.

*Thornwater, ninety-third. The healer.*

"Hands," said Cael.

There were five. He drew the cream sheet toward him, and folded it once along its old fold, and put it inside Hesk's book for the night.

He wrote by the same lamp, when the others had gone down to the meadow.

*Ninetieth day. Lowmarch. The seventh line is said: seventy in the purse, sixty-four on the wall. Tenth. The smith is paid, and we have a hundred and eight. A rope is owed to the ferryman, with no figure. Two holds have asked for us by name, and will wait a fortnight.*

*Twelve weeks. Seven contracts. Pike says there's ground out here where the Arbiter goes quiet, and nobody goes in twice. Karis has been waiting her whole life for a sentence like that. Seln says the contract was written by someone from inside. I say it pays. Two days east, through Thornwater, on the day the healer said. For twelve weeks nothing out here has read me. I'd like to see what a place that reads nothing does with that.*

*The nine: held. Deployment since the Line: one.*

---

He gave the paper back to Pike in the morning, across the oak, before the mist was off the river.

Pike took it without a word. He read it twice, though he could have said it in his sleep, and then he turned his shoulder to the meadow and gave it out: the recovery, and the Fallow road, and the rise, and the hundred. It was the first time in a month that those words had been heard on that grass. He said the whole of it a second time. Then he said *taken*, and five names, and dipped his pen.

Cael counted who heard it, since a thing said at that plank was only as good as its hearers.

Teague was there. He stood a little way out from the eave, on the trodden grass, with his thumbs in his belt, and his three behind him at their distances: the spear, grounded, and the bow on the good leg, and the boy with a heel of bread. That made four. The Oxhollow carter had come up from his mules and stood chewing, which was five. The hide-woman had her arms folded. Under the ash the old carter had lifted his chin at the first word and was holding it there. Two men in tarred aprons had stopped with a bundle of shingles between them; and the boy who took the standing-money stood on one leg beside a tent-rope, with his bag forgotten in his hand. That was ten.

The eleventh was at Pike's elbow. He was a thin young man with ink on his cuffs and a ledger under his arm, and he had come up from the landing at a trot when he was sent for.

"You're the witness for the hirer," said Pike.

"I am told so." The clerk looked at the five as if they had been bales of a kind that he had not handled before. "My master had it by the last sack, sealed, with the sum counted in a bag and a line of direction. He was to post it and to lodge the price. Whatever is brought down, I am to see it at the landing and put our mark to the slip, and it goes west by the next boat." He swallowed. "I don't know what the things are. I was not told who wants them."

"Nobody's asked you," said Pike, and wrote five short strokes at the foot of the cream paper, side by side.

"What if they're not there?" said Lira. "The things. Somebody else might have picked them up. It's been lying under your stone a month."

"Then you come down and tell me so, and I say that." Pike was blowing on the strokes to dry them. "I write what was done. There's a hundred in my box against his mark on this paper, and if his mark never goes on it, the box stays shut. I've nothing in the book that pays a crew for walking."

"So we could go four days and come home with a line and no money."

"You could. You've read my lines. Every one of them says what came back."

It was done in the time that it takes to lace a boot. Cael waited for something to come from the trodden grass, and nothing came. The man with the leather on his wrist had watched the pen make its strokes. He did not nod, by so much as the width of a finger, as he had nodded on a river bank; he stood with his eyes on the paper until it was folded, and then they moved to Cael, and rested there, and told him nothing that he could have written down.

There was one thing more, and Cael had turned it over in the night.

"The two holds," he said to Pike. "With our names. We'll be nine days on this, if it goes as it's written. If we're not down in their fortnight, don't keep a bull waiting on us."

"I'd not have. I told them a fortnight, and after that the top of the sheet." Pike blotted the strokes. "It's good of you to say it where he can hear."

He had not said it for that. But he saw, from the corner of his eye, that the boy with the bread had stopped chewing, and was looking at his captain.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-26.md

# Chapter 26 — White Paper

The ferry brought its sack at noon, and Pike gave out the mail as he gave out everything.

There were nine pieces, and the meadow had come up from its five fires to hear them; it stood about the eave in a loose half ring, leaning a little each time the one hand went into the canvas. The smith had one. The hide-woman had none this month, and went away with her jaw set, and a fold of bark tied with red thread was held up for the ferryman, twice, until a boy ran with it.

"*Cael,*" said Pike. He was frowning at a sheet folded very small. "And under it, *past the Line.* I've had that direction once before." He looked over the heads, twice. "I've the one Cael."

Cael went up for it.

"Stand where you are," said Pike, and his hand was in the sack again. "There's another. Same name, same three words, and a different hand altogether."

So he walked back down the grass with two letters, and thirty people watched him do it.

He knew the first by its writing before he had it fairly in his fingers. It was a narrow upright hand, every letter the same height, and he had read it on a yard's rota slates for two years, and once at the foot of a bout sheet. The second was a square of stiff paper ruled faintly in red, folded in four and tied with a waxed thread. It was a leaf out of a ledger, and he knew of only one person who would have cut a page from her own book in order to write to him on it.

He had sent one letter west to her, on the fourth day, by a courier with a split lip, and it had carried no place to answer to; to the other he had sent none at all. Both of them had written into the air, as an old man in Denvash had, and the air had brought both to the one plank that there was.

"Well?" said Lira. She had come up on his left. "Aren't you going to open them?"

"Not here."

"The river, then. You went down to the river with the last one."

"I had the last one read in a quarter of an hour, three times over, and then there was nothing left of it." He turned the red-ruled square in his fingers. "I've had one letter in ninety days. Now I've two, and four days of road in front of me. I'm going to have one of them at the wash tomorrow, at the noon halt. I'll keep the other for a noon with nothing in it."

Lira stared at him.

"You're rationing your post."

"He's quite right," said Brom, with feeling. "I cut a loaf and a half into fifteen on a mountain, and it was the only thing that week that came out even. A letter's no different. You don't eat it all the day it comes."

"It's a letter. It doesn't go off."

"It does, though," said Cael. "The first reading goes off. You only get the one."

He put them both inside Hesk's book, between the leaves, where a cream sheet had lain the night before. It was a small thing to have decided; but he had gone a long time with nothing in front of him except what he was holding down, and he liked the weight of two unbroken seals a good deal more than he would have liked knowing what was under them.

They spent the rest of that day getting ready for a road. The Oxhollow carter had his off mule at the smith's for a shoe. Brom went over the three box-ropes a hand's breadth at a time, since they were all the rope that the five now owned. Seln was gone from the meadow between the mail and dusk, and came back with pale dust on his boots, and said nothing; and Cael, who had made a promise at that plank, looked at the dust and then at the fire.

---

They were at the wash by noon of the ninety-second day, with the cart behind them and the saddle behind the cart.

It lay as it had lain on four other crossings, dry and red, thirty yards from lip to lip, with a ramp cut down either side and the thorn stump standing on its eastern edge. The carter took his mules through at a walk and halted them on the far flat to blow, and the five sat down among the chock-stones where they had twice made a camp.

Cael took out the sheet that was folded small.

"Aloud," said Lira. "If it's his. I want to hear how he sounds on paper."

"It's mine."

"I know whose it is. I stood on a yard with him for two years, and I'd like to know he's well, and you'll only give me the half of it afterward in your own words." She pulled her knees up. "Read it as it's written."

He looked at the others. Karis had her chin on her fists, and Brom had stopped with the bread halfway out of the bag; and Seln, a little apart as always, had turned his head.

Cael broke the wax, and read it as it was written.

*Cael —*

*Weather first, because you'll want it in order, and I've watched you want things in order.*

*The Crown yard has a new cohort captain. The intake came up the bluff a fortnight ago, forty-one of them, and they've put a third-year over them who was a second-year when you left and whose name you would not know, so I'll not spend the ink. He is fair. He counts slower than you did. They stand where he puts them.*

*Withrow's tenure holds. That is the whole of the sentence. I asked whether there was a longer one and was told there was not, in a way that meant there had been, for a week.*

*A query came down to the registry from somewhere above it. Bracken would not say where and I did not ask twice. It wanted former enrollees' last known direction of travel. Bracken answered it. He gave them a map reference to the Line and nothing past it — which is true, and which Bracken enjoyed being true. He told me so at the plaque wall with his hands behind his back, and then told me he had not told me.*

*I still lose to you every time I run it. I have stopped running it in the yard.*

*Rooke says the yard's quieter. He didn't say it was worse. He also didn't say it wasn't.*

*— E.*

A small wind came down the wash and lifted the corner of the sheet, and it was some little time before any of them found a word to put after that narrow upright hand.

"Good."

It was Lira, and she had said it between her teeth, with great satisfaction.

"What is?" said Brom.

"Bracken. A map reference to the Line and nothing past it. Somebody up a stair wanted to know which way we'd gone, and he gave them a place that everybody already knew we'd been, and wrote it out properly, I expect, with a number." She was grinning. "I never liked him. I'd like to shake his hand."

"He'll have enjoyed it for a week," said Karis. "It is the only kind of joke that a registrar is allowed." But she was smiling too, in her small tight way; and then she sobered. "Withrow holds. I had wondered. They had a week of something, by that, and it went no further."

Brom had put the bread down, and for a little he only looked at it.

"He's quoted Rooke," he said at last. "That's Rooke to the life. He'd not tell you a thing was good, and he'd not tell you it was bad; he'd tell you what he'd seen, and you could carry it home and find out." He looked at his own hands. "*Quieter.* I'd like to know which of us he meant."

"All of us, I should think," said Lira. "You broke more benches than I did."
