Cael had read the last lines but two again, to himself. *I still lose to you every time I run it.* He could see the bout as it had gone, on sixty feet of sprung oak, with a blade's edge declared against him in earnest and nothing held back on either side; and he could see a tall, exact person walking it through afterward in an empty yard, alone, looking for the place where it turned. Something in Cael stirred at the thought, quick and bright, and asked *Edge?* of the nearest arris, which was the lip of the wash. He told it no. It was Ephram's, that question. He carried it for him, and paid the rent on it, and had never yet spent it.

"Read me the fourth again," said Seln. "The query."

Cael read it. Seln heard it out with his eyes on the ground, as he listened to anything that he meant to keep, and at the end he nodded once, and asked for nothing more.

"Does it bear?" said Cael.

"I do not know yet. When I do, I shall say."

Cael folded the sheet along its creases. He had two things now, and he laid them side by side in his head, as Karis had once laid a noun beside six numbers. Somebody above a registry had asked which way certain people had gone; and a paper had come over a river with a hundred marks, written by a hand that had learned its trade at a ruled desk, sending whoever took it two days east of a fence.

He did not join them. It would have been very easy, and he had been joined to things himself on no better evidence, by people with seals. Two points could be ruled together by anybody. He would wait for a third.

---

Karis had the printed sheet out that evening, for the first time since a shelf above a valley with no name.

They had camped short of the lower crossing, on a halting-ground that they knew, and Seln had a fire going. She took the roll from the middle box herself, and carried it to a patch of bare beaten earth where the last of the light fell, and unrolled it there on the ground with a chock-stone on each of its corners; and beside it she laid the calfskin.

"Come and look," she said. "I want it seen by more eyes than mine. I have carried this for ninety-two days and not opened it once, since I knew what was on it."

They stood round it, and it was a handsome thing. The registry had printed it from a plate, in black, on a sheet as long as Brom's arm, and the whole of a continent was there: every district with its bound ruled round it, and every chartered road a firm double line, and the towns in three sizes of letter by their standing. Cael found Ardenmere, and a river he had crossed on a bridge, and a bluff above Ostrand with a small square on it. Somebody had written *ridge* beside a pass in the east, in pencil, and a little farther on, *Shelf; a pine; water at 36*, in the same small exact hand.

A hand's breadth past that pencilled word, the plate ran out of things to say.

"There's the Line," said Karis. Her finger went down a ruled stroke that crossed the sheet from top to bottom. "And here is what they have printed east of it."

It was a river or two, laid in without their bends, and a scatter of hills, drawn all alike; and it was seven roads. They left the Line at seven places down the length of the sheet, and each was drawn as a row of short dashes, with no name beside it; and each ran out into the white paper for the length of a thumb, or a finger, and stopped. There was no town at the end of any of them. The dashes simply came to an end, as a person's voice does who has forgotten what he meant to say, and beyond them the sheet was clean to its margin.

"Seven," said Lira. "Which is ours?"

"I make it the third from the top, by where it crosses the first water. They have drawn the crossing and left out the ferry. They have left out Lowmarch." Karis put her little finger on the last dash of the third road. It ended in nothing, three fingers east of the river. "That is about the saddle. By this sheet we walked off the end of everything that is known the first time we climbed out of that meadow, and we have been standing on blank paper ever since."

"He called them that," said Brom. "The old man, at the plank. Void roads. I didn't know what he meant by it."

"It is a clerk's word," said Seln. He had come up behind them. "An entry is void when nothing has been written against it. The road is granted; it is on the sheet. It has no particulars. I should think that a carter was shown one of these once, by somebody who had come over with it in his coat, and asked what the dashes were, and went home with the answer."

Karis had turned to the hide.

It looked a poor thing beside the print. It was scraped calfskin, brown at the edges, drawn in oak-gall and burnt stick by a man who could not write his name; and it had on it every ford between the river and the eastern holds, and every hold as a square with a dot, and the days as ticks.

"Now his," she said. "I have been over this again since the plank, and I have found two things that I had read wrongly. I took them for his thrift."

She showed them. The first they knew: it was the double line that left its straight, two days beyond Thornwater, and went round a patch of bare skin and came back, and she had ringed the patch in pencil.

"That is the only place where one of his roads walks round a blank. I said so on the fifty-eighth day, and it is still so. But he draws a hill with short hatching, and water with three wavy strokes. Look here, south of the ring with the bar, a day down and a little aside. He has hatched hills all across this corner, close as thatch, and in the middle of the hatching there is a round place the size of my thumbnail where he lifted the stick."

There was. Cael would have taken it for a thin place in the hide.

"And here, a long way south-east, among his water. Strokes all round, and a round of bare skin in the midst, with no road within a day of it." She had ringed both. "Three blank circles. One I can source, since a one-armed man has named it. The other two are a man lifting his hand from a skin. I have written *guess* beside each, and I shall not call them anything else."

"Could you rule a line through them?" said Lira. "You've got that brass thing."

"On this? No. It is a map of days. He has drawn a day's haul the same length uphill and down, and bent his roads to fit the skin. I could lay an edge across it and learn something about the calf." She settled back on her heels, and her eyes went from the one to the other, the black print and the brown hide, lying side by side on a carters' halting-ground.

"Their map has no roads. His has no quiet ground. Between them you get the country."

She rolled the printed sheet up again before the dew could reach it. Cael noticed that she rolled the calfskin second, and more slowly.

---

They came up to Thornwater's gate an hour after noon on the ninety-third day.

The ford had been only a ford. Its white stones ran out along the lip of the sill to the bar, a yard apart, without a gap, and there was nothing on the gravel but a wagtail; he had looked down the bar to its tail all the same, and at the bend below, where two had gone on the fifty-third evening and not been seen since. They took the stretch at noon, a mile of pale grass with the frost still lying in its roots and a white stone standing in it, and he walked it with his hands at his sides and nine things shut.

On the platform the bowman lifted two fingers from his stave. Lira put up the great boot, an inch, and set it down.

Then the bar was drawn, and they went in under the lintel, and there was a bench against the inner face of the fence that had not been there before.

It was only a plank on two kegs. A small brown mule stood beside it with her pack still on and her head down, and on the pack, lashed along its top, lay a long shape in an oiled cover. Astride one end of the plank sat a carter whom Cael had not seen before, a heavy man with a grey stubble, who held his right hand out in front of him, palm upward, as a person holds a hand out for coin.

A square woman in a brown coat sat on the other end, and she had the hand between both of hers.

Her eyes were shut and her lips were still, and the sweat had come out along her cropped hair in the cold; and Cael understood that she had come through that gate herself not long before them, and had sat down to this without so much as slipping a pannier. She had owed it for fifteen days.

They stood and waited, and it was not long. She drew a slow breath and opened her fingers, and the carter turned his palm to the light. There was a pink seam across it from the root of the thumb to the heel, dry and shut. He closed the hand, and opened it, and closed it again on nothing, as a man tries a tool that has come back from the smith.

"That's one," said Oryn.

She said it to the fence. Then her eyes came round, tired and level, and went over the five of them from a stride and a half, a single time, and stopped on Cael's left side.

"Fifteen days. Since the break."

He had not opened his coat. She had got it from how he stood, and from a sum that she had plainly done on the road.

"Breath stops at it."

It was what she would want first, and so he gave it first: the tape was where she had laid it, and he had learned the edge.

"Ninth day. I said nine. You've counted."

"So did you."

"I count days. It's the only thing I do count." She got up off the plank by pushing on her knees. "I eat now, and I'm on my back two hours, and then there's a yard of people. You can tell me where you're going while I've a bowl in my hand. Five don't walk up this valley in the cold to keep an appointment about three ribs."

He told her at the long house door, with the steam of a barley broth going up between them.

He did not make a sentence of it. He gave it as he would have given Pike a line: a paper off the plank, under a broker's seal; something to be fetched from a place on the Fallow road, two days east, at a rise; out by the east gate at first light. He said what the place was called, and what Pike called it, and he asked her for nothing. He had no more right to that sentence than he had had at a well, and he found that it was easier to leave unsaid the second time.

Oryn ate, and heard him out, and scraped the bowl.

"The ring," she said. "I know it. I've been round it thirty times, with the mule, by the ruts." She set the bowl on the sill. "You broke on the seventy-eighth. It's twenty-one days by the rule, and I don't lift a tape on a guess, so that's the ninety-ninth. The long one wants a morning with nothing else in it. That's the hundredth. I'd meant to sit at this gate's table from tonight until then; the grain's moving, and that bench fills as fast as I empty it."

She was doing it aloud, as she had done it over a girl's foot on a hillside.

"Two days out. Two back. Whatever you spend there. That has you at this gate again by the ninety-ninth, if nothing goes wrong, and I've walked behind you people long enough to know what *if* is worth." She held up a thick finger. "That's one reason. I want a patient whole and still for the long reading, and I've seen what your crew brings home from a road. I'll not have him back here on the ninety-ninth with something new broken, and wait three weeks more."

"And the other?" said Karis. She had come up without a sound.

"The other's mine." Oryn looked at her own right hand. "Every carter on my round has told me the same thing about that place, for thirty rounds. That a healer's hands are no good in it. Not one of them's seen it. They had it from their fathers. I've let it go by me all that time, since I'd no business in there and a list to keep." Her mouth went hard. "I don't like a thing about my own hands that I've only been told."

She took up the cheek-strap, and let it fall again.

"Six gates wait on me the same days, whether I spend them at this table or behind you on that road. I know who's at every one. It's this bench that goes short, and I'll pay it when I come back through." Then she looked straight at Cael, as she had looked at him once across a hollow of shale. "You're a stop. I've added you."

He could find nothing to say that was the size of it.

"Don't make it a long stop."

She went in to sleep.

He stood at the long house door with an empty bowl on the sill beside him. She had a round of seven gates, and she was late at every one of them already, and nobody had asked this of her; and she had just spent five days of it on a road that every carter she had ever mended told her to keep off. He had wanted to say the sentence for nine days. He was glad now, with his whole heart, that he had left it for her, since from him it would have been a request, and from her it was a decision.

---

From the loft door, that evening, with his legs hanging over the yard, he watched the healer work.

It was the place where he had written on his first stay at Thornwater, and on his second. Two of the boxes stood behind him among the barley sacks, and the third, with the corner that was worn white, waited by the ladder's head for the morning. Down in the yard the Oxhollow carter was backing his mules to an empty bed. He would load grain at first light and go home by the ferry, sixteen days in all, and he had said goodbye to nobody; but Cael had seen him stand for some time looking at a boot that hung from his cart's tail, and then untie it, and carry it over, and stand it carefully outside the barn door.

The steading's woman had given the healer her back room without being asked; and when Karis inquired whether that was the custom, she had looked at her as at a person asking whether the well had water in it.

"It's hers when she's here," the woman said. "It's nobody's when she's not."

There was a bench under its window, and by dusk there were seven people on it, three of the hold and the rest come in with the east carts, and one of them a child. Oryn came out when the shadow of the fence had crossed the well, and washed to the elbow, and went along them as he had seen her go along eight at Oxhollow; and she chose two.

That was no longer new to him, and he did not count her lips; he watched the yard instead, where nobody stood about. He had seen a healing house's corridor once, from a bed, and it had been full of people waiting to be told their turn by tier. Here the five whom she had passed over got up from the bench without complaint and went back to what they had been doing, and the woman of the hold set a lamp on the back room's sill, unlit, and a bowl beside it.

Oryn glanced at the lamp, and then across the yard.

"You. Ember."

Karis was on the well's coping with her notebook. She looked up.

"I told you a week, on a mountain, and three days on top of it at a ford. That ran out on the eighty-seventh, and this is the ninety-third. Have you asked it for anything since?"

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because I have been on roads, where one does not. And because—" Karis stopped. She did not often stop in the middle of a sentence. "Because I looked for the place, the first night, and it was gone. I had sooner not look again and find it gone a second time."

"That's honest." Oryn jerked her head at the sill. "This is a yard, and I want a light. Come here."

Karis got down, and crossed the yard with her hands held a little away from her sides, as she had carried them when they were bound, and stood before the lamp. Cael saw her draw a breath. Then her right hand made the smallest dip that he had ever seen it make, as a person sets a full stop.

A seed of yellow stood on the wick.

It was no bigger than a grain of barley; it caught, and the flame came up round it, ordinary and smoking, and the seed was gone into it, and Karis let out her breath in something that was not quite a laugh. He had last seen her do that across sixty yards of mountain, with her hands shaking like a rope under load.

"Somebody has sewn the pocket back into the coat," she said.

Oryn had two fingers on her wrist already, and two on her neck, and her lips moved to ten.

"Three parts full. It'll be the whole by the end of the week." She let go. "You spent it to the last copper once. Don't do that again where I'm not."

She turned to the first of her two, and the lamp shone on her hands, and after that there was nothing to see but a woman kneeling.

Cael sat on in the loft door until the cold drove him in. Tomorrow there would be an east gate, and a road with a bend in it; and for the first time since he had crossed the Line, six people would go out by a gate together where there had always been five.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-27.md

# Chapter 27 — The Rise

Karis made her long cord along Thornwater's fence in the first grey light, with Brom to do the knots.

Two of the three boxes were to stay in the loft, and so two of the three box-ropes were free. Brom married them end to end with a splice that he worked by feel, and hung the whole length from a fence-post with a stone on its end to take the stretch out, while she stood by with her short cord, the one that was knotted at every foot. When the rope had done creaking she laid the short cord along it again and again, and at each length she tied in a scrap of red wool from the hold's mending basket. Cael watched her count. She told nobody the figure, and wrote it in her notebook with her shoulder turned, as a person writes down the weight of his own purse.

"It's a cord now," she said, coiling it. "It was two ropes, and they are not to be thought of as ropes again until I have done with them. Nobody is to tie a box with this."

"What do I tie the box with?" said Brom.

"You carry it. You have told me for a month that carrying is a different thing."

The middle box stood open on the barn floor, and she had been at the others since before he woke. She had the lid off the third. He came up the ladder in time to see her lifting out the masonry chips, each in its twist of paper with a number inked on the twist, and laying them in a row on a sack; and when the last was out there was a flat canvas bag at the bottom of the box, sewn shut and sealed, with five thumb-marks in the wax.

She looked at that for a breath. Then she put the lid on over it, and drove the pegs home with the heel of her hand.

"Those stay," she said. "The chips come. I have a piece of every sort of dressed stone that I could reach between Denvash and the Line, and a date for each, and if there is so much as a doorstep at this place, I mean to know which of them it matches."

So the middle box went east on Brom's back, with the chips in it, and the lamp, and the calfskin and the printed sheet, and a quire of clean paper. He knelt and had it set on him, and passed a strap of plaited sacking over his chest, and stood up. He had refused to let anyone take a turn. Cael had seen that look on him at the edge of a moss.

Oryn was waiting at the east gate with the mule.

She had the two panniers on, and the hold's woman had roped a roll of six grey blankets across the pack, behind the long shape in its oiled cover, with instructions that they were to come back. The healer stood on the mule's far side from the hill. She had slept, by the look of her, and she gave the five of them one glance from her stride and a half, each in turn, and seemed to find nothing in it that wanted her hands.

"You'll walk at her pace," she said. "It's the only one I've got."

The bowman had come along the fence to that gate. It was not his; he kept the western one, and Cael had never seen him ten yards from his platform. He stood with his bow at the length of his arm and looked at the road going up the valley, and then at Lira, and he lifted two fingers from the stave.

Lira had a boot in each hand. One was the carter's left, which she had found standing outside the barn door at first light. She hung it on the mule's pack by its laces, with great care, against the other one going.

Karis stopped under the lintel. She took the printed sheet out of the box on Brom's back without asking him to kneel, and unrolled the eastern edge of it across her forearm, and found the third of seven dashed roads with her finger.

"It ends here," she said. "At about this fence, or an hour beyond; the plate isn't fine enough to say. I have carried this sheet for ninety-four days in case it should be of use, and from this gate on it is a clean piece of paper."

She rolled it, and put it back in the box.

They came to the fork an hour out. The valley divided there round a spur of hill, and one road went away up the left-hand water toward a smoke that he could just see. The right-hand branch climbed to a low place in the skyline and went over. It was a road like any other, two pale ruts with a ridge of grass between. There was nothing at its mouth to say what it was.

"That's the Fallow," said Oryn, and clicked her tongue to the mule.

---

He read the second letter at noon, on the far side of the hills, since it was a noon with nothing in it.

They had halted on a level of short turf where a spring came out. Brom had been helped off with the box and lay on his back beside it with his arms spread. The others had gone to the water. Lira had not; she had sat down next to Cael, and stretched out both legs, and was looking at Hesk's book where it lay on his knee.

"You've still got one," she said. "I can see the thread."

"I was going to read it now."

"Read it to me. You read me his."

He drew out the square of stiff paper. The waxed thread was tied in a flat knot that he remembered from the corners of a ledger, and when he slipped it the leaf opened along two folds. It was ruled in faint red, in columns, for figures. She had written straight across the columns, in a hand as plain as print.

He read it aloud, and it did not take long.

*Filed with your record. It reads: left in good standing, undefeated on paper. I've kept this ledger forty years and never had a line like it. The session still stands. The road still runs through Ardenmere. — V.*

Lira began to laugh before he had reached the end.

"*Undefeated on paper.* I've had you on your back with a lath more times than I've had breakfast."

"She doesn't keep a column for laths."

"No. I don't suppose she does." She took the leaf from him and read it through again herself, with her lips moving, and Cael watched her come to the end of it and go back two sentences. "She's got a book like the old man's, hasn't she? She writes down what happened. And she's put you in it as a thing that she's never had to write before."

"She had me in it before anybody would give me a number. I asked Pike once whether he'd heard of her."

"Had he?"

"He'd never heard of the town."

He had thought about that on the forty-ninth night, and it came back to him now on a turf bank two days past a fence. There was a woman in Ardenmere who kept a straight ledger of a fighting yard, and a one-armed man in a meadow who kept a straight book of a plank; and neither had the least idea that the other was alive. He was written in both. He did not think that any registry on the continent could have said as much of one of its own.

Lira had her finger on the last line but one.

"What session?"

He did not answer at once.

"I know what she's speaking of. I don't know what she means by saying it now, on a ledger leaf, to somebody who's past the Line." He took the paper back, and folded it along its two creases. "It's hers. I'd tell you if it were mine. I only keep it."

"You're not going to guess."

"I've been guessed at."

She let that be. It was one of the things that she did, and that almost nobody else had ever done for him. She leaned back on her hands and looked up the road, where it ran on eastward over the turf, as straight as a ruled margin.

"She puts the thing that decides it last."

He looked at the leaf. *The road still runs through Ardenmere.*

"So does Ephram."

"He put Rooke last."

"He put what Rooke wouldn't say last. It comes to the same." He tied the thread again, in the knot that it had come in. "They've each of them written to tell me that a door's open, and neither would be seen holding it."

"That's two," said Lira. "And an old man who wants to hear that you've a roof. You've more people behind you than you had when you'd a bed to sleep in."

He put the leaf inside the book, beside the other. There was nothing left now with a seal on it. He did not mind that either, since both of them had turned out to be the kind of letter that keeps.

---

The Fallow road ran east for the rest of that day, and it was the emptiest country that he had ever walked.

There was grass. That was nearly the whole of it. It began at the height of his ankle, on the turf by the spring, and by the middle of the afternoon it stood to his knee, and before dusk to his hip: pale stuff, the colour of dry straw, with a seed-head that hissed against his coat. No stock had cropped it. There was not a fence in sight, or a smoke, or a tree, only long slow swells of ground going away to a sky that seemed to begin at his boots. The wind came over it from the north with nothing in its way, and laid the whole of it over in one direction, like the nap of a cloth under a hand.

"Why does nobody graze this?" said Brom. He had the box up again, and he spoke without turning his head. "There's feed here for a thousand head."

"The holds either side won't put a beast on it," said Oryn. "I've asked. They don't give you a reason. They say it's fallow, as you'd say a field was resting, and then they talk about something else."

"For how long has it been resting?"

"Longer than anybody I've had my hands on."

They walked it quiet. That was the hold, as it was on any road, and here it needed no telling. Seln went fifty paces ahead and never once looked back. The ruts were plain enough; the grain carts had used them within the month, and here and there he found the mark of a small neat hoof, and beside it, on the low side, a boot.

He watched the healer walk, since it had become a habit. She kept the carter's pace. She was below the mule on every slope, though the slopes were so gentle here that he could not always have said which side was the low one, and she changed over without seeming to think. He set himself to her beat, and breathed to the tape, and for long stretches of that afternoon the tape had nothing to say to him.

They had a fire at dark, in a fold of ground, lit with Seln's flint.

"One more night of that," said Seln, feeding it. "I should not light one tomorrow."

"Because of the dogs," said Lira.

"Because I do not know what is there, and a flame is seen a long way over grass." He sat back. "It is the same answer."

Oryn had taken off her boot to look at her heel. She pressed the strip flat with her thumb, and then, with the boot still in her hand, she looked across the fire at Brom.

"Give me that wrist. The one I did."

He gave it, and she laid two fingers on the inside of it and two on his neck, and her lips moved to ten.

"That's there," she said. "It's sound, and it's yours, and I'd know it in the dark by now. I'll want it again the day after tomorrow, at this ring, so don't do anything to it."

"What for?" said Brom.

"To see whether I lose it." She let him go. "I can't tell whether my hands have stopped unless I've got them on something I know. I'm not putting them on a stranger's ribs at a place like that and guessing."

Karis had her notebook open, and her pencil was not moving. Cael knew what that stillness cost her. A primary source was sitting across a fire from her, pulling on a boot, and had been round the place thirty times.

"What do the carters say?" she asked. "I had it from you at the long house door, in a sentence. I should like it as they give it, if you'll tell me. I shan't ask twice."

"They say a healer's no good there. That's the words. *You'll not want her in the ring; she's no good there.* I had it first from a carter at the first gate I ever mended at, and I took it that he meant I'd be frightened." Oryn laced the boot. "Then I had it from six more, in the same words, and not one of them a man to be frightened of a field. So it's a thing that was seen once, by somebody, and handed down."

"Seen how? A healer went in, and tried to mend, and could not?"

"That's what it'd have to be. Nobody can tell me who, or when, or what was broken." She shrugged, heavily. "A hold keeps what it needs of a thing. It needed *don't*. It didn't need the rest, so the rest is gone."

Karis wrote that down, and underlined nothing. He saw her look at what she had written, and then at the dark beyond the firelight to the east, with an expression that he knew. She had been given a rule, and under the rule there had once been a finding, and somebody had thrown the finding away.

"The day after tomorrow," she said, half to herself, "there will be six of us who saw it. I mean to write down who."

---

They came to the rise late on the ninety-fifth day, with the sun low at their backs and their shadows going on ahead of them up the road.

It was a long swell of ground, no steeper than a ramp, and the ruts climbed it in a straight line. Seln had stopped at the top. Cael saw him against the sky, a narrow dark figure with his hands behind him, and he did not move as they came up. When they stood beside him they saw why.

The road turned. He had walked it for two days as straight as a plumb-line, and here, at the crest, both ruts swung out to the left together, toward the north, in a wide easy curve, like a stream going round a rock; and a long way off, beyond the curve's far end, he could see them come back and go on eastward along the line that they had left.

Straight in front, where the old line would have run, there was only grass. It lay over in the wind, unbroken, down a gentle slope and up another.

On that second slope stood the ring.

It was three hundred yards off, as near as he could judge. There were grey stones standing on end, close together, in a rough circle; he counted them, and had eleven. No two were of a height. One was as tall as two men, and the one beside it came no higher than a knee, and all of them ended in a break. Between their feet lay something pale and level that he could not make out.

"That's it," said Oryn. She had halted the mule. "I've never been nearer than these ruts."

Cael looked at the ground between the turn of the road and the stones, and it told him nothing. There was no ditch, and no bank, and no change in the grass. A carter of twenty-two years had drawn that curve on a calfskin and put nothing inside it, and Cael could not have said, standing where the man must have stood, how the man had known where to bend his wheels.

"I should like to learn where it begins," said Seln, "before I lie down beside it. There is perhaps an hour of light."

"Nothing declared," said Karis quickly. "By anyone. That's the hold, and it is my condition as well; I want to know what is felt, and I can't know that if somebody is doing something at the time." She was looking along the old line. "We go abreast, an arm's length apart, at a slow walk, so that we all come to it together. If you feel a thing, put your hand up, and say what it is like. Don't say anything before."

"And if we feel nothing?" said Brom.

"Then we shall have walked to a ring of stones, and a one-armed man has been wrong for thirty years."

They left the mule at the turn, with her rein under a rock. Brom knelt out from under the box and set it beside her. Then the six of them spread across the old line, in a row, and Cael took the right-hand end. Lira was next to him, and then Brom, and Karis, and the healer; Seln had the far end, on the north.

They walked east.

He counted strides, since somebody would want the number. And he did what a one-armed man had told them to do, though it had not been said to him. He turned his attention inward, to the place behind his breastbone where a small bright thing had gathered on the morning of his Kindling. He had gone looking for it a few times since, in the first months, on bad nights. He looked now. It was as it had always been: a dark place, like a niche in a wall where a lamp once stood lit.

*Seventy-eight. Seventy-nine.*

At the eightieth stride Lira's hand went up.

She had stopped dead, on both feet, as she had taught herself to stop on a gravel bar. Her head was a little on one side.

"It's like the air got thick."

Brom had halted in the same instant. He was frowning at the grass in front of his boots, and his big hands had come a little away from his sides.

"Like a door closing behind me."

Cael looked along the row. The healer had not spoken at all. Both her hands had risen, half way, with the palms turned up and the fingers spread, as a person holds them out to learn whether it has begun to rain; and she was staring at them.

"My Arbiter's gone."

That was Karis. Her voice was quite level, and her face had no colour in it whatever.

"Back up. Ten paces. Tell me when it comes back."

Seln had not raised his voice. He stood at the north end of the row with his hands at his sides, for once, and Cael had never before heard him give an order as though he expected to be obeyed.

They stepped back.

"Back," said Lira; and Brom said it with her, and Karis, and the healer, and Seln himself, a breath behind the rest. It was five voices on the one word. Not one of them had taken a second step.

"Same stride. All of us."

Karis was looking down the row, from face to face. Then she went forward again by herself, a foot at a time, with her hand held out in front of her as though she expected to touch something; and at a certain place she stopped, and drew back her toe by the width of a hand, and put it out again. She bent, and felt about in the roots of the grass, and came up with a grey stone the size of a loaf.

She set it down on the place.

Cael had gone forward the eighty strides with them, and back the one. He stood now on the near side of a stone, with five people who had each lost a thing and had it given back, and nothing in him had altered at either step. The dark place was dark. He had looked at it going over and looked at it coming back, and he could not have told the two apart.

The nine fragments lay in him where they had lain since a ford. He was sure of that as well. When Lira's hand went up, Wind had leaned in his own feet to go with hers, as it had leaned every day for two years, and he had put it down without thinking; and that had been on the far side of where the stone now stood. Anchor was asking about the stone already. *There?*

He did not know what it meant.

And nobody had turned to him. He saw why, and it was the simplest thing in the world. All four of them knew what he was. They had known for years that a brass plate had waited eleven seconds over him and gone dark, and that nothing had spoken to him out of that dark since. A line that put out a lamp had nothing in him to put out. It had not occurred to one of them to ask whether he had felt it, any more than they would have asked a man without a purse whether he had been robbed. The fifth knew nothing of brass plates, and she was looking at her own two hands.

So there were two reasons, and he looked at each of them squarely, standing in the long light. He did not know what he would be telling them. And he had not been asked.

Neither one was a good enough reason by itself. He knew that. He kept silent on the pair of them together, and his own shadow lay out in front of him across a grey stone and on into the grass.

---

He wrote it at the stone, kneeling, while there was still light enough to see the page.

*Ninety-fifth day. The Fallow road, at the rise. Eighty strides east of where the ruts turn.*

*Quiet ground. Karis's Arbiter stopped at a line she can mark with a rock. So did Lira's, Brom's, Seln's, Oryn's — same line, same step. Mine didn't. Mine's been stopped since I was fourteen. I don't know what that means. Nobody asked. Tomorrow we go in.*

*The nine: held. Deployment since the Line: one.*

He read it over. It was short, and it was set down as he would have set down a count, and under it he could feel the size of what he had not put.

They made their camp at the turn of the ruts, eighty yards back from the stone, and there was no fire. Seln had chosen the place. It lay a little below the crest on the western side, so that a person lying down could not see the ring and could not be seen from it. They ate cold bread from Thornwater's oven, and the hold's blankets went round, and the mule stood with her tail to the wind.

"Two and two," said Seln, "and nobody alone. I shall take the first with whoever will have it."

"I will," said Brom.

The mule had settled the matter of the line for herself an hour before. She had been left with her rein under a rock, and she had drawn it out and grazed her way down the old road; and when Oryn went to fetch her she was standing with her forefeet on one side of the grey stone and her hind feet on the other, pulling at the grass, as easy as a beast in its own paddock.

"She doesn't know it's there," said Lira.

"She's nothing in her for it to take." Oryn had the rein, and she stood looking at her animal's four small hooves with an expression that Cael could not read. "I've walked her round this place thirty times, by the ruts, because I was told to. I never once thought to ask what she'd have done if I'd let her choose."

Shadow lifted then, as it lifted at every dusk. Cael felt it come up soft behind the other eight, as it had come up beside a turf fire on the fifth day and at a ford on the fifty-third, offering to take him out of the light; and he told it no. It had come at the turn of the ruts as it came anywhere. He wondered whether it would have come a stride beyond the stone, and thought that he knew, and did not go to see.

He walked up to the crest once more before he lay down, since he wished to see the place with the day going off it.

The sun was under the western hills. The grass had gone grey, and the eleven stones were black against the last of the sky, and all the long slope between had lost its colour and lay like ash. Only one thing in that whole country was holding the light. It was the pale level thing among the feet of the stones. He still could not have said what it was, at three hundred yards. It lay there like a pool, and the last light stood on it as light stands on water after the banks have gone dark; and it went on shining, faintly, for a long while after it should have stopped.

He was not the only one who had come up to look.

The healer was at the stone. She stood on the near side of it, with her boots together, like a person at the edge of a pond. She had both arms stretched out in front of her at the height of her waist, so that her two hands were over the line and the rest of her was not, and the palms were turned down.

She kept them so. He did not go nearer, and he did not speak. He watched a square dark figure stand at a stone with its hands held out over nothing, as still as the uprights beyond, until the pale thing on the far slope had faded and there was no light left anywhere for her to see them by.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-28.md

# Chapter 28 — Sixty-Two Cords

That same night, in the second watch, Karis went back to the stone.

She did not go alone, since Seln had made a rule and she had lost no time in seeing the sense of it. Lira had the watch with her. Lira sat down in the grass ten yards short of the place, with her back turned and her knife across her knees, to look at everything that was not a ring of stones; and Karis went on the last ten yards by herself, with a notebook, and a pencil, and no lamp.

There could be no lamp. She had lit one in a yard two evenings ago and been absurdly glad of it, and she would not have set a point here for all the paper in Ternhall. She knelt down in the frozen grass on the western side of the stone and laid the notebook open on her knee. The stars were out. She could see the white of the page and not much else, and she resolved to write large, one line to a leaf, and sort it by daylight.

First she called her sigil.

She had been taught to do it at fourteen, by attending to a place behind the breastbone, and it had been years since she had needed to do it on purpose; it was simply there when she wanted it, as a clerk is there behind a counter. It came now. It hung at the edge of her sight where it had always hung, small and steady and yellow-white, and it had about it the particular quality of attention that she had never been able to describe to anybody who did not have one.

She looked at it for a good while. Then she stood up, and stepped over the stone.

It went out.

There was no flicker in it, and no fading. She had half expected a lamp turned down, and it was a lamp that had never been in the room. She stood a stride inside the line with her heart going hard, and looked for the place behind her breastbone, and found the place. There was nothing in it.

She stepped back, and it was there. It hung where it always hung, with the same steadiness, as though it had been waiting behind a door and had not noticed that the door was shut.

Karis did it nine times.

She counted them, and at each crossing she made herself attend to one thing only, as she would have made a student attend. The third time she watched for the instant, and it had no width that she could find. The fifth time she went over slowly, a hand's breadth at a time, to learn whether it dimmed; it did not, and then it was gone. The seventh time she tried to hold it. She fixed the whole of her will on that small bright mark and carried it forward as one carries a full cup, and it went out between one heartbeat and the next without the least sense that anything had been taken from her. That was the part she liked least. She had not been robbed. She had merely looked down and found her hands empty.

After the ninth she knelt again, on the near side, and thought about emptiness, since she had lately become something of an authority.

She had been empty for nine days. She had spent every point she owned on a mountain in eight seconds, and afterward she had not been able to strike a flint. But in all those nine days the sigil had been there. She had called it on the first night under the pale rock, and it had come, faithful as ever, and when she asked it for a point it had simply had nothing to give her. That had been a counter with a clerk behind it and an empty till.

This was a different thing altogether. A stride beyond that stone she was three parts full; a healer had told her so with two fingers on her wrist. The till was heavy. There was no clerk, and no counter, and no room for them to stand in.

*Empty: sigil present, reserve gone.* She wrote it large, by feel. *Here: reserve present, sigil gone.* She turned the leaf. *These are not the same condition and must not be entered under one word.*

Then she set about the stride.

It was the only measurement that she could take tonight, and she took it with the stubbornness of a person who has been given one tool. From the turn of the ruts to the stone it was eighty of Cael's strides, by his count, and his were a yard. She paced it herself, there and back, and her own count was a good deal larger, as it always was. She wrote both. She paced it a second time and was one stride out, and wrote that too, and the mean of the pair.

She tried next to make the line finer than a stride, and could not.

A hand held out across the stone did nothing at all; the sigil hung where it was. She had watched the healer stand so at dusk, for a long while, and had not asked her what she felt. An arm did nothing. But when she stood astride the stone itself, with one boot on either side, she had the sigil or had it not according to how she leaned, and Karis would not write down a lean. She put: *Narrower than one stride of mine. No finer figure tonight. Wants daylight, and a second person to watch my feet.*

She sat back on her heels. The grass was stiff with frost, and beyond the stone the ground went down and up again to eleven shapes that she could only just tell from the sky.

It was very tidy, and she made herself look at that, because it was what she was afraid of.

Five people had walked up to this place in a row, an arm's length apart. She was an Ember, and Lira a Wind, and Brom an Iron Skin; there was a Shadow among them of a higher tier than any of the rest, and a Tide who had been trained in a healing house. Karis had sat at lectures on what an Arbiter was. She had copied out, in a restricted room, a good many pages on the subject, and on one point every page agreed: it was a person's own. It woke for that one person, on one morning, and named a single gift.

And five of them had gone out on the same stride.

Her pencil had come down on the page. She looked at the point of it, resting on white paper in the starlight, and she knew exactly what kind of sentence it was about to begin. It would have had *as though* in it. It would have said what the thing was like; and a likeness, set down in a notebook at midnight, is a hypothesis that has not yet had the honesty to call itself one.

She lifted the pencil.

*Five rows,* she wrote instead, and ruled them by touch. *K. L. B. S. O. Each: gone at the stone, going east. Back at the stone, coming west. One stride. Observed by all five at once on the 95th; by me alone, nine times, that night.* And under that, because she kept that sort of account as well: *I am frightened by how well it agrees. Entered.*

There were six of them on that road, and she had ruled five rows. She considered that. It was right as far as it went; one could hardly enter the stopping of a thing that had stopped years ago, on a brass plate, in front of witnesses. She left a space at the foot of the leaf all the same. She had no idea what she expected to put in it.

Behind her, Lira shifted in the grass.

"Well?" It was hardly a whisper. "Is it still there?"

"It is in exactly the same place," said Karis. "To the stride. I have never in my life measured anything that kept so still."

---

She gave them the procedure at first light, with the frost smoking off the grass, and she gave it once.

"We are going to walk round it," she said. "I want to know what shape it is and how far about, and I can't see it, so I shall have to find it sixty times with my feet. Lira goes a stride inside, the whole way. She is the instrument. She says nothing unless it comes back to her, and then she says *out*, and steps in again."

"Why me?" said Lira.

"Because yours sits nearest the skin of any of us. You had your hand up before the rest of us had finished the stride." Karis shook out the long cord, with its scraps of red wool. "I walk on the line itself, with this end. Brom has the other, at the last mark. When the cord comes tight between us I stop, and find the line under me, and that is a station. Brom cuts a turf there with the bill and turns it over, so that we leave a black mark in the grass at every cord. Then he comes up to me, and we do it again."

"And I'm in front," said Seln.

"You are in front, reading whatever is on the ground, and you will stand where I wave you. He writes." Her pencil pointed at Cael. "The number of the station, and one figure more, which I'll give him."

"What do I do?" said Oryn.

"You keep the stone. Somebody must be standing at the place where we began, so that we know it when we come round to it from the other side. And I should like one of us to be outside the whole time, with the mule, where an Arbiter is an Arbiter."

The healer nodded. She had expected nothing else, Cael thought, and she took up her post beside the grey stone with the rein in her hand, as she would have stood at the head of a bench.

They set off northward, with the ring on their right hands and the ruts some forty yards away on their left.

It went slowly at first. Lira walked with her arms a little out from her sides and a look of deep concentration, like a person carrying a bowl filled to the brim; twice in the first cord she said "Out," sharply, and stepped to her right. Karis felt her way along beside her. At the cord's end she stopped, and shuffled a hand's breadth inward, and a hand's breadth out, and stood still.

"Here."

Brom came up, and drove the hooked point of the bill into the turf, and levered out a square of it as big as a plate. He laid it grass downward. It showed black on the frost.

"One," said Cael, and wrote it.

At the second station Karis did a new thing. She turned round and looked back along the cord to the first turf, and beyond it to the stone; and then she waved Seln forward along that same line, until he stood a full cord ahead of her with a peeled wand held upright.

"That is where the next mark would fall if the line ran straight," she said. "It won't. Watch."

They walked the third cord. Lira kept to her stride inside, and Karis to the line, and when the cord came tight Karis was standing well to the right of Seln's wand. She had her short cord out already, and she laid it on the grass from the wand to her own boots and read the knot.

"Six feet and a half. Inward. Write that beside the three."

He wrote it.

He had put down sixty-three lines at her dictation on a mountain, and this was his third on a hillside, and it was of another kind from any of those. On the bench he had written what a cord and a clock could show to anybody who came after. Here the only instruments were two people's Arbiters, going out and coming back. He had none to offer, and so he held the pencil for the people who had; and he did not grudge it, which surprised him a little.

---

It was Brom who found the iron.

He had come up to cut the third turf, and he set the bill's point where Karis's heels had been and leaned on the haft; and there was a sound that did not belong on that hillside, steel on iron, a short dull clink. Brom took the bill away as if it had burnt him.

"There's a thing in the ground," he said.

It stood a boot's width from the place where she had stopped, and the grass had hidden it. When Brom parted the stems with the side of his hand they all saw it: a square bar of iron, as thick as two fingers, driven straight down into the turf so that a hand's length of it stood clear. It was brown with rust and flaking, and its head had been beaten a little flat, and spread at the edges, by something heavy.

Lira had come out across the line to look, and she crouched and tipped her head.

"There's a mark on it. On this side. And figures."

The mark was stamped into the iron, a thumb's breadth below the head, and Cael knew it before the rust let him see the whole of its shape. He had seen it painted over a hearth in a wool-factors' counting room, and cut in a lintel at the last station on a western road; it was printed in the corner of the sheet that lay rolled in the middle box, back at the turn of the ruts. Under it somebody had punched two numerals.

"Thirty-one," said Karis.

She had gone very still, with the cord's end in her fist.

"That's the registry's stamp," said Lira. "That's theirs. What's it doing in a field two days past a fence that isn't on their map?"

Seln had come back down the line with his wand, and he stood over the stake and looked at it without bending. His mild face told Cael nothing whatever, and by now Cael knew that for a kind of news.

Something in Cael had turned toward the thing as well. Anchor had asked about doorways for a year, and about every sill and rim on a mountain; it asked now, more quietly than it had ever asked, as one craftsman takes note of another's work. *There.* It was not a question. Here was a point that somebody else had fixed, and told to stay, and it had stayed.

He put that down, and looked at what was left.

He had laid two things side by side at a wash four days ago, and had refused to rule a line between them for want of a third. A query had come down to a registry from somewhere above it, asking which way certain people had gone. A paper had come over a river with a hundred marks, in the hand of somebody who had learned to write at a ruled desk. And here, on the edge of a place where a carter would not put a wheel, was a bar of iron with a registry's stamp on its neck.

That made three. He could rule the line now, and it would be straight. What he could not do was say which way along it he was looking.

"Don't touch it," said Karis. Her voice shook a little, and then steadied. "Nobody is to pull at it, or clean it. I have three stations and I want sixty. It has stood there a good while without us and it will stand until we come round." She turned her back on it with a visible effort. "Brom. Cut the turf a foot short, and I'll allow for the foot."

"You're leaving it," said Lira.

"I am finishing a count. If I stop at every wonder on this hillside we shall be here at dusk with half a shape, and I had rather have a whole one."

They went on. Cael walked the fourth cord with his head turned back over his shoulder. The bar stood in the grass where they had left it, brown and small, beside a square of black turf; and he thought that it was a strange thing to have crossed a Line, and a mountain, and ninety-six days of road, and to be met at the far end of all of it by two numerals that somebody at a counter had signed for.

---

By the tenth station she no longer needed the wand.

"It is in my wrist," she said. She was standing with the cord's end held out before her, and as Cael watched she swung it to the right, without looking down, and stopped. "There. Lira?"

"In," said Lira, a stride beyond her.

"Measure it."

He laid the short cord from where the straight line would have run to where her boots were, and it was six feet and a half.

"It has been six and a half ten times," said Karis. "My hand has learned it; I bring the end round by the same bend at every station, as one learns the turn of a stair in the dark. I shall go on measuring, since a wrist is not an instrument. But I know already what he is going to write."

He wrote it at every station after that, and it never altered by so much as a knot.

They went round the north side with the ruts beside them. He could see where the carts had gone, forty yards off, keeping the same distance from the line the whole way, as exactly as if the carters had had a cord of their own; and he supposed that in a sense they had. A man who walked in front of a mule here for twenty-two years, with a Path in him, would have learned to the yard where he ceased to be comfortable.

From the north the ring looked different. The eleven uprights had closed up and overlapped, and between two of the tallest he had his first clear sight of what lay among them. It was a floor, grey and pale and perfectly level, raised a little above the grass like the top of a low table; and the morning light lay on it without a shadow.

The ruts came back to their old line on the eastern side, and went away from them over the next swell. Seln was waiting there, a cord ahead, and he was down on one knee.

"Here," he said, when they came up. "And there, and beyond that tussock. I should like all of you to see it before it is trodden."

There were paths in the grass, and Cael would not have found them. They were no wider than his two hands, and the stems had not been broken, only pressed aside, low down, by something that went under them rather than through. Three of them crossed the line within twenty yards of one another. Where the nearest ran over a patch of bare ground there was a print in the frost-softened earth, as broad as a palm, with no mark of a claw.

"Going in?" said Lira, very low. "Or coming out?"

"Both. That one is this morning's, and it points inward. This is older, and it goes out. They use these as a hold uses its gate." Seln had not risen. "I cannot give you a number from a path. I can say that it is not one animal, or two. And I can say that I have gone the whole of the north side and not found a single run. They come and go by the east."

"He told us they lived in them," said Brom.

"He did. I had hoped that he was repeating something." Seln stood, and dusted his knee. "I have seen no animal. I should like that entered exactly. I have seen where they walk."

Cael looked at the print, which was the breadth of the ones beside a white stone in a valley, and of the ones on a gravel bar; and then he looked at the sun, which was a third of the way up the sky.

"We go in at noon," he said. "Not before; it's the hour they've never once been seen abroad, on a stretch or at a ford. And we're out again and on the west side of that stone with the sun two hands above the hills. Whatever isn't done by then isn't done."

"Two hands," said Seln. "I shall hold you to it."

They took the south side at a better pace, since by now each of them had the work by heart. Lira drifted out twice more and said so. Brom's turfs lay behind them in a long curve, black on the pale grass, each a cord from the last, bending always by the same small measure to the right; and when Cael looked back along them from the fiftieth, he could see the curve closing on itself like a hoop.

From the south he had the whole of it in one view, for the first and only time. The grey stone was away on his left hand, with a small brown mule beside it. Beyond it the ruts swung out round the north in their wide curve. The floor lay in the middle of everything, with its eleven broken stones, and on the far side, where no turf had been cut, there was the low place in the grass where Seln had knelt. Nothing moved in the whole of it but themselves. It looked like the plainest piece of country in the world, and five people had just walked the edge of it with their Arbiters going out and coming back at every stumble.

---

The last cord fell short.

They had come up from the south, with the grey stone in plain sight ahead and a square brown figure standing beside it with a mule. Karis reached the end of the sixty-second cord, and stopped, and found the line, and Brom cut his turf; and the stone was still some way off.

She coiled the cord over her shoulder, and walked the rest on the line, in her own short steps, counting under her breath, until she set her last foot down against the stone itself.

"Sixty-two cords and twenty strides," said Karis.

She stood there with her eyes shut, and her lips moved for some time. Cael knew what she was doing, since she had the length of that cord in feet, to the knot, in a notebook that nobody else had read, and had paced her own stride twice against his in the dark. He would have given a good deal to check the sum; he had only her figures for the half of it, and so he waited.

"One thousand three hundred and seventy-four yards, and a piece."

"Round a field," said Brom.

"Round a field, with the same bend at sixty-two stations out of sixty-two, and it has come home to the stone it started from." She opened her eyes. "That is not a rough round. I have the offset written sixty-two times in his hand, and it is one figure. There is only one shape that turns by the same measure at every step and closes."

She turned her head and looked across the grass to the east, where eleven broken stones stood about a pale floor.

"From any mark on it to the middle is ten cords. I can work that without a table. Ten cords from this stone, on the old line of the road, and one would be standing in the centre of those."

Then she said one thing more, without any weight, as she had once read out the width of a shelf.

"A circle is a made shape."

It lay in the air a breath. Lira was looking at the stones, and Brom had the bill over his shoulder and was counting his turfs. Oryn, who had stood at that stone since first light, was watching five people come toward her along a line that she could not see. Nobody took the words up, and Karis did not seem to look for anyone to do so. She had entered a property of the figure, and she bent to tie the cord.

Cael heard it. He put it with the three other things, a little apart from them, since it was of another kind.

Seln had already started back along the black marks toward the third.

Cael went after him, and they stood over the iron together. In the full light it was plainer than it had been: the square bar, and the spread head, and the stamp. Seln crouched now, without laying a finger on it, and looked at the rust, where it had lifted in leaves, and at the turf round the bar's foot, which had grown up close against the metal on every side.

"Five years in the weather. A cold term either side."

"You can tell that from rust?"

"From rust, and from the grass. It has had time to forget that anything was done to it." He tilted his head to bring the stamp into the light. "I have seen iron of this pattern given out across a counter, and signed for. A man answers for what he takes."

"Thirty-one."

"I do not know what the number counts, and I shall not guess." Seln rose. He stood with his hands behind him and looked from the stake to the ring, and back along the line of turfs to the stone, as he would have looked over a room that somebody had left in a hurry. "It stands a boot's width from where she stopped. She found that place with her feet, this morning, knowing nothing. Whoever drove this found the same place to the same boot's width."

"With a cord," said Cael.

"With a cord, or a chain. That is what I should like you to carry away." He nodded at the iron. "You drive them where you've measured to. Not where you've measured from."

Cael looked at it. A stake marked the far end of a measure; somebody had stood at another place, with the other end of a line in his hand, and had paid it out until it reached exactly here.

He looked along the only line there was. It ran from the stake's foot, across two hundred yards and more of pale grass, to the middle of eleven stones.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-29.md

# Chapter 29 — A Stride Inside

They held the trials at the stone, at noon, and Karis set the order.

"Smallest first," she said. She had ruled a fresh leaf into five rows and given it to Cael, with the pencil. "A one-armed man has told us that nothing can be declared in there, and five of us felt our Arbiters go, and I believe both. I believed in a ledge as well. There are prints in the grass on the far side of this field, and I am not going to learn that he was mistaken by having Lira crack the sky open a quarter of a mile from whatever left them."

"I'd not crack it on purpose," said Lira.

"You did not crack it on purpose the last time. That is rather my point." Karis looked round at them. "So I go first, with one point, unlit. If it sets, I shall know it, and nobody else will, and we stop there and think. If mine does not, then Brom. Then Seln. You are last of the four, and you go only if the three of us have had nothing."

"And outside the line nobody tries anything," said Seln. "It is a road. I should like that kept."

"Nobody tries anything outside. We know what we are outside."

The sun stood as high as it would go at that time of year, pale and small, and their shadows lay short on the frosted grass. They had gathered on the western side of the grey stone, with the turn of the ruts at their backs and the ring two hundred yards and more in front. Oryn had the mule by the cheek-strap a few yards behind the rest, and her free hand tucked into her armpit for warmth; she had watched the whole of the morning from that stone without a word.

Cael looked at the leaf that he had been given. There were five rows on it, each headed with a letter, and his own was not among them. That was as it should be, since he had been handed the pencil for the very reason that there was nothing of his to enter. He had kept the count at a great many trials of other people, from the east tier of a yard, and had thought himself used to the seat.

Karis stepped over.

Cael saw her shoulders tighten as her foot came down, and ease again; she had done this nine times in the dark, she had told them at first light, and it plainly did not grow pleasanter. She stood a stride inside the line, with her back to the ring, and her eyes went slowly along the slope in front of her from left to right, as he had seen them go over a district floor before a bout.

"I can see them," she said. "Every place. I could put sixteen down on that bank and tell you where each would sit, to the hand. That is not altered in the least."

"That's only knowing how," said Brom.

"It is knowing how, and it is still here; I want it written that it is still here." Her right hand had come up. "Now I am going to set the first, eleven paces off, by that tuft with the frost on it."

The hand dipped, the small exact dip of a person dotting a page.

Nothing happened that he could see, and that proved nothing, for a set point gave no light; so he watched her face instead, and it had the look of somebody who has put a foot down for a stair in the dark and found the floor.

"No," said Karis. She tried it a second time, and a third, quickly. "No. It has not set. A lecturer would say that the declaration did not render. And that is not the whole of it. When I am empty and try to set one, I feel myself reach into the purse and close my fingers on nothing. Here I am not even reaching. I am three parts full, and I can't find the hand to reach with."

"Does it hurt?" said Oryn, from behind the stone.

"Not in the least."

"Does it tire you? Trying."

Karis thought about that with real care.

"It costs nothing. There's nothing to spend."

She stepped back over. He saw the colour come into her face as the foot came down, and her eyes go, for an instant, to something at the edge of her sight that nobody else could see.

"Back," she said. "First row. Write it as I gave it: every place seen; none set; no cost."

He wrote it, and looked at it a breath longer than a line needed. He had sat in a whitewashed room for the better part of a year while that small exact person laid points on a wall for him to study, and he carried one of them still, the fifth of his nine, with its rent paid; and he had not known until this noon that there was a place anywhere where her hand could make that dip and have nothing come of it.

---

Brom went over with his sleeves rolled.

He had done that on purpose, Cael thought, so that all of them should be able to see his forearms: the left with its pale dry seam from the heel of the hand to the middle, and the right, which had never had a mark on it. He stood a stride inside and held them out a little before him, with the palms down.

For forty-three days he had refused that Path, and Cael had watched him do it on a ferry, and in a wash, and under a cart on a crust of rock. He had seen the grey start at those wrists whenever a load came on suddenly, and each time he had seen Brom put it down again, like a man pressing a lid onto a pot; it had been the hardest kind of work, and it had been done every hour.

Now, for the first time since a river, Brom told it to come.

"Skin."

He said it aloud, in his ordinary voice, as he might have called a dog that was lying a yard off.

They waited, and the big brown wrists stayed the colour of wrists. He turned them over to look at the insides, and turned them back.

"Well," said Brom.

He did not seem distressed; he stood there with his arms out, frowning a little, like a person who has walked into his own kitchen and found a chair moved.

"What's it like?" said Lira. "You said a door, yesterday. Closing behind you."

"It's that. The door's shut, and I'm on the wrong side of it from where I keep the thing." He let his arms fall. "It's queer. I've not used it since the ford, and I'd have told you I'd got used to going without. But I always knew where it was. I could feel it in the next room, as you can feel a fire through a wall. There's no next room."

He looked about him, and his eye fell on the middle box, which sat on the grass just outside the line where he had put it down. He leaned across the stone and took it by its two beckets, and lifted it over, and stood holding it against his chest.

"Same," he said, after a moment.

"The same what?" said Karis.

"Weight. I wanted to know whether a hundredweight was a hundredweight in here, with nothing behind me." He set it down inside the line, quite gently. "It is. So that's what I've got. Write that."

"I am to write that a box weighs the same."

"It's the only thing I've tried that worked."

Cael wrote it. He wrote *Skin called aloud; none*, and under it, since the row was Brom's, *lifted a hundredweight; no difference*; and it seemed to him that of the two findings the second was the one that Brom would take home.

Seln had been standing at the end of the row with his hands behind him.

"I shall be brief," he said. "I have very little to show."

He stepped across, and for a breath he only stood, with his eyes half shut. Cael knew what he was reaching for, since he carried a piece of the same thing himself, sealed, and had felt it lift in him at every dusk for two years.

"There is nothing to lift," said Seln. "I looked for it as one feels for a banister. It is not that it resists. It is not there to be asked."

"Then you're only a man in a coat," said Lira. "Like the rest of us."

"I have been only a man in a coat for a great deal of my working life. Will you all look at the mule for a count of three, and then look back?"

It was an odd request, and they did it. The mule was regarding a tussock; Cael counted three, and turned his head, and the place where Seln had stood was empty.

There was the grass, and the short noon shadows, and a stride beyond the stone a patch of bent stems where two boots had been. Lira made a small sound. She had the best eyes of the five, and Cael saw them go the whole length of the slope and come back with nothing.

"Four yards to your right," said a mild voice. "And down."

He was crouched low against a tussock of the pale grass, side on to them, with his face turned down into the crook of his arm and his hands drawn up into his sleeves. He had put himself to the south of them, where the low sun came from behind him into their eyes. There was no Path in it, and no trick that a child could not have understood once it was shown; and it had still taken five people, all of them looking for him, the better part of a breath to find a man in a dark coat on an open hillside at noon.

He stood up and brushed the frost from his knee.

"I've a knife and fifteen years. It was never most of what I do."

"That isn't a trial of anything," said Karis. But she was looking at the bent grass, and at where he had got to, and her pencil hand had twitched. "I suppose that it is a trial of you. Third row. *Path sought; none. Practitioner not found for a count of two.*"

"A count of three," said Seln, coming back over the stone. "I should not like the record to flatter you."

---

Lira went last of the four, and she did not make a joke of it.

She sat down on the grass outside the line and took off the great boot, and unwound the whole of a spare shirt, and stood up on her own taped foot with the frost under it. She was to run on nothing for a fortnight yet, and nobody reminded her of it; this was one stride, and she had plainly weighed it against a healer's month before she touched the lace.

"I want all of it," she said. "Not a lean. I'm going to ask for the whole thing, the way I'd ask on a card with a panel watching, and I'm going to ask along the line, that way, north. So if the old man's wrong I come down in grass and not on top of one of you."

"And if he is wrong," said Karis, very quietly, "it will be heard at Thornwater."

"I know what it'll be. I heard it come back off a mountain." Lira's face was pale. "It went off by itself, that morning. I never told it to. I've got to walk into a place with dogs in it on this foot, and I'd like to know, before I do, whether it can."

There was no arguing with that. Cael had the ruling, and he gave it with a nod, and she stepped over the stone and turned to face north, with the ring on her right hand, and set her feet.

He had watched her do that a thousand times, from a rail at Norhold, and on a gravel bar in the mist, and at the mouth of a ledge on the seventy-eighth morning. He knew the order of it as he knew his own name: the weight going forward onto the balls of both feet, and the heels lifting, and then the gathering in her calves that came a breath before the air broke. For three months he had seen her reach that last place and refuse it.

She did not refuse it now. He saw her ask.

Every sinew in her answered. Her whole body went into it at once, and in his own feet Wind leaned to go with her, as it always did; he put it down.

She took one step.

It was a long one, and very fast, and it carried her a yard and a half along the line; she landed on her left foot and brought the right up beside it, and stood. The grass hissed, and that was all the sound there was. Over the whole wide hillside the air lay as it had lain before, cold and still, and no echo came back from anywhere, since nothing had been sent.

Lira stayed as she was, looking down at her own two feet on the frost, a yard and a half from where they had started.

"That was a step," she said.

"I asked for all of it. I did everything. You saw me; you'd have seen if I'd held any back." She lifted her head. Her eyes were very wide, and for an instant Cael thought that she was going to weep, and then he saw that it was not that at all. "It's in me. It's not in the air."

She turned it over, standing there.

"I'd always thought of it as mine. They told me at Fenmark that it was a thing I *did*, and I believed them, and I was proud of doing it well. And every piece of the doing's still here. I can feel it. I could do that step again exactly." She set her right foot forward on its whole sole, and drew it back. "There's just nothing out there to take hold of."

"Come out," said Oryn. "You've a bare foot on frost."

Lira stepped back over the grey stone, and stopped dead on the near side with her breath caught.

"Oh," she said.

"Back?" said Karis.

"Back. All at once." She had her hand flat against her own breastbone. "I felt it at the stone last night, but I wasn't looking then. I was looking this time."

"What is it like? I want it in your words. I have Brom's door, and my own purse."

Lira frowned, and hunted for it.

[[TIER-B: Lira, of her Arbiter's return on stepping back over the line — "like a clock that stopped and started again without losing the time." — coordinator to supply protected text]]

Karis had Cael write that down as it was spoken, the whole of it, in the fourth row. She read it over his arm when it was done.

"That is the best of the four," she said. "It is the only one with a measurement in it."

Lira wound the shirt on again, with fingers that were not quite steady, and pulled the great boot over it, and stamped.

"I've spent three months not doing it," she said, to the boot. "I thought that was hard. It's nothing to standing there and asking with everything I've got and being given a step." She looked up at Cael. "I know what's left now. When it isn't me doing the *not*."

"What?"

"Legs," said Lira. "And they're good ones. Somebody took trouble with them."

---

"Now me," said Oryn.

She had given the mule's rein to Seln, and she came up to the stone at her own pace, rubbing her hands together, and looked at Brom.

"The wrist. The one I did, that I read at the fire."

He held it out. She took it between both of hers, as she had taken a carter's palm on a plank at Thornwater, with two fingers of her right hand laid along the inside where the pulse was.

"This is how it goes," she said. She was speaking to Karis, since Karis was the one who would have it written. "It's the short reading; it costs me nothing. I'll have it running, and I'll count aloud so that you can hear where I am. We go over together, him and me. Then back. Then I'll want a third, and I'll tell you what when I've had the two."

"You have never done this," said Karis.

"Nobody has that I ever met. That's why I walked two days."

Her lips began to move, and this time there was sound in them. "One. Two. Three." She nodded to Brom, and the two of them stepped over the stone together, the big man and the square woman, hand in wrist. "Four—"

It stopped. It did not trail away; it stopped as a word stops when a door is shut on the speaker. She stood a stride inside the line with Brom's forearm between her palms, and her mouth was a little open, and there was no count in it.

Cael had seen that look once before, on a mountain, over his own ribs.

"Out," said Oryn hoarsely.

They stepped back, and her lips moved at once. "One. Two." She went all the way to ten this time, with her eyes shut, and Cael saw her shoulders come down as the last of the count went by.

"That's there. It's his, and it's sound, and I could tell you what he had for breakfast." She opened her eyes. "And a stride that way I'd my hands on a piece of warm meat."

"Is it Brom?" said Karis. "Forgive me; I must ask it. His Arbiter stopped at that stone, and yours did. If the thing that you read is his current, and his current is shaped by his Path, then perhaps there was nothing in him to read."

"That's the third." Oryn had not let go of the wrist. "You. Stand where you are, this side. Give me the arm across."

Brom stood on the western side of the stone, where he was an Iron Skin with his Path in the next room. He stretched out his left arm. Oryn kept her two hands on it, and she stepped over the stone by herself, so that she stood inside and he outside, with the mended wrist held between them across the line.

Her lips did not move at all.

She stayed so for a count of twenty, and Cael kept it for her, since she could not. She shifted her fingers on the wrist, and pressed, and shifted them again; then she let go of Brom with her left hand, and bent, still holding him with the right, and laid the left on her own shin.

She straightened.

"Nothing. Not nothing in him. Nothing in my hands."

The wind went over the grass. Nobody at the stone had anything to put beside that.

"He's standing out there with everything he owns. I had it not a minute ago. And I can't so much as feel that he's alive, except that he's warm." She looked down at her two broad palms, as she had looked at them in a hollow of shale, and this time there was no puzzlement in her face. "They told me, and I never checked. Thirty rounds."

"Then the carters were right," said Karis.

"A healer's hands stop working there."

She stepped out. The count came back to her lips before her second boot was down, and she let it run to ten on Brom's wrist, with something like greed, and only then released him.

"So you'll all hear this, and you'll hear it once," said Oryn. "I'm coming in with you. I said I'd see him whole to the ninety-ninth, and I can't do that from a stone. But understand what you've got. If one of you puts a leg through something in there, I can pull it straight. I can splint it, and I can carry my end of you. I can't mend it. Not a scratch. Not till you're this side of that." She pointed at the stone with her chin. "Whatever happens in there happens to six people with no healer."

Cael looked at her as she said it. She had walked two days up an empty road to learn that the one thing she owned in the world, besides a route, would be taken out of her hands at a stone; and having learned it she was going in regardless, with her sleeves pushed back, to be of whatever use a pair of ordinary hands could be. He had not asked her to come. He did not think that he would have had the face to ask it, knowing what a stride had just cost her, and he saw that she would have come all the same.
